## assistant

Yes, I can see all of it. I only read files and changed nothing. The questions are in `projects/lsatu/public/data/`, on the latest commit `190ff7b` from 2026-09-25.

**Logical Reasoning**
- 156 section files, covering PT101 through PT159 (`PT###-S#-LR.json`).
- 3,965 questions in total.
- Each question has the stimulus, stem, answer choices, correct answer, question type, reasoning type, difficulty and question features.
- None of them is missing its stimulus or answer choices.

**Reading Comprehension**
- 60 test files, PT101 through PT159, in `public/data/rc/`.
- 320 passages and 2,150 questions.

**Explanations**
- The LSAT Lab write-ups are in `Explanations/` for PT139 through PT159, as markdown.
- 22 JSON versions are in `public/data/explanations/`.

**Other files**
- `_bank.generated.json` indexes all 156 LR sections.
- `question-stats.json`, `finder-address-index.json` and the RC indexes are also there.

One thing I haven't checked: the files on disk could differ from what students see on the live site. The app's content lives in Supabase (Postgres), not in these files, so I haven't confirmed that the database matches them.

import { useQuery } from '@tanstack/react-query';
import { supabase } from '@/integrations/supabase/client';

/**
 * Authored LSAT Lab explanations, read from the `lr_*` tables.
 *
 * These are transcribed verbatim from the source and verified against LSAT
 * Lab's own API (see `ai-os/wiki/explanations/COVERAGE-AUDIT.md`). Nothing here
 * is generated: if a field is null the source did not provide it, and the UI
 * must render that absence honestly rather than filling it in.
 *
 * Coverage is deliberately partial. Roughly 350 of the ~3,770 ingested
 * questions have no authored prose at all — LSAT Lab published only a video
 * for them. `hasAuthored` is the flag callers should branch on; that is the
 * signal for falling back to the AI tutor.
 */

export type LrStyleKey = 'single' | 'standard' | 'brief' | 'another_way';

export const LR_STYLE_LABELS: Record<LrStyleKey, string> = {
  single: 'Explanation',
  standard: 'Standard',
  brief: 'Brief',
  another_way: 'Another Way',
};

/** Display order for the style selector; mirrors LSAT Lab's own ordering. */
const STYLE_ORDER: LrStyleKey[] = ['standard', 'brief', 'another_way', 'single'];

  questionNumber,
}: LrExplanationQuery): Promise<LrExplanationData | null> {
  if (pt == null || section == null || questionNumber == null) return null;

  const { data: question, error: qErr } = await supabase
    .from('lr_questions')
    .select('id, preptest, section, question_number, question_type, correct_choice')
    .eq('preptest', pt)
    .eq('section', section)
    .eq('question_number', questionNumber)
    .maybeSingle();

  if (qErr) throw qErr;
  if (!question) return null;

  const [choicesRes, explRes] = await Promise.all([
    supabase
      .from('lr_answer_choices')
      .select('letter, choice_text, tag, student_percent, is_correct')
      .eq('question_id', question.id)
      .order('letter'),
    supabase
      .from('lr_explanations')
      .select('id, style')
      .eq('question_id', question.id),
  ]);

  if (choicesRes.error) throw choicesRes.error;
  if (explRes.error) throw explRes.error;

  const explanationRows = explRes.data ?? [];
  const explanationIds = explanationRows.map((row) => row.id);

  // Steps and notes are fetched for every style at once, then grouped, so the
  // style selector switches without another round trip.
  const [stepsRes, notesRes] = explanationIds.length

## assistant

You have **548 Logical Reasoning questions with no explanation**. That's 354 found in the live database plus 194 that aren't in the explanation tables at all. Another 298 have only a partial one. I only read data and changed nothing. RC isn't checked yet.

This comes from the live LSAT U database (the `lr_*` tables the app reads explanations from), queried just now.

| Status | LR questions |
|---|---|
| In the explanation tables | 3,771 |
| **No explanation at all** | **354** (352 with no row, plus 2 with a row but nothing in it) |
| **Partial:** notes on each answer choice, but no walkthrough of the argument | **298** |
| **Never loaded into the explanation tables** (in your 3,965-question bank, but not in these tables) | **194** |

The app's own code says about 350 have no written explanation because LSAT Lab only published a video for them. That matches the 354.

**Where the gaps cluster** (these counts combine the missing and the partial ones):
- **PT154 is missing all 77 of its questions.** That looks like a failed load rather than video-only questions.
- PT148: 33 of 76.
- PT101–PT122 run 9 to 37 per test, so most of the older gaps are there.
- From PT124 on, most tests are missing between 0 and 6.
- PT156 has only 15 questions loaded at all.

**Not checked yet:**
- **Reading Comprehension.** It uses a different storage path.
- **The 194 questions that were never loaded.** I only compared totals, so I haven't listed them by test.
- **Which of the 548 are video-only.** I can't tell yet which ones have a video on LSAT Lab and which were simply never transcribed.

Want the full question-by-question list saved as a file, and RC checked the same way?

## assistant

I made both files. They cover **846 Logical Reasoning questions** that are missing an explanation, have only a partial one, or were never loaded into the explanation tables. I only read the live database and changed nothing there.

**Files**
- [wiki/lr-missing-explanations.md](wiki/lr-missing-explanations.md) lists the questions by practice test, then section, then status. Each line reads like "S3, Missing: Q2, Q15, Q16". There's no question text.
- [wiki/lr-missing-explanations-by-category.md](wiki/lr-missing-explanations-by-category.md) breaks the same questions down by question type, reasoning type, feature and difficulty, with a Missing / Partial / Never loaded split for each. It ends with a row-per-question table.

**Totals**

| Status | Count |
|---|---|
| Missing (no explanation) | 354 |
| Partial (notes on each answer choice, no walkthrough) | 298 |
| Never loaded (in your question bank, not in the explanation tables) | 194 |

**What the analysis shows**
- **The gaps follow the common question types.** Must Be True / Most Supported has 125 gaps, Flaw 122, Necessary Assumption 81, Paradox 80, Principle 78 and Parallel 60.
- **Some types lean one way.** Method (42 of 56) and Parallel (40 of 60) are mostly missing outright. Weaken (45 of 65) and Strengthen (31 of 42) are mostly partial, so each answer choice has a note but there's no argument walkthrough. Those two need different fixes.
- **Reasoning type:** comparative reasoning comes up in 335 of the 846, causation in 226 and conditional in 225. Another 195 have no reasoning type tagged in the bank.
- **Most common features:** Plans/Recommendations (124), Rebuttals (120) and Missing Link (91). Two-speaker questions (78) are heavily in the fully missing group: 54 of them.
- **Worth checking first: two tests in the never-loaded group look like a load problem, not a writing gap.** Most of PT159 (37 questions) and PT156 (36) were never loaded. Both already have transcribed explanation files in `Explanations/`, so loading them could close most of those 73 without writing anything new.

**Things to know**
- The question-type labels in your bank are inconsistent, with about 60 spellings for roughly 15 real types (for example "Must be True" and "Must Be True"). I grouped them into families in their own table and kept the raw labels in a second table. The grouping is my call, so check it before you rely on it.
- I also added both pages to `wiki/index.md` and put an entry in `wiki/log.md`.
- Reading Comprehension still isn't checked.

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\Joshu\.claude\skills\domain-modeling

# Domain Modeling

Actively build and sharpen the project's domain model as you design. This is the *active* discipline: challenging terms, inventing edge-case scenarios, and writing the glossary and decisions down the moment they crystallise. (Merely *reading* `CONTEXT.md` for vocabulary is not this skill: that's a one-line habit any skill can do. This skill is for when you're changing the model, not just consuming it.)

## File structure

Most repos have a single context:

```
/
├── CONTEXT.md
├── docs/
│   └── adr/
│       ├── 0001-event-sourced-orders.md
│       └── 0002-postgres-for-write-model.md
└── src/
```

If a `CONTEXT-MAP.md` exists at the root, the repo has multiple contexts. The map points to where each one lives:

```
/
├── CONTEXT-MAP.md
├── docs/
│   └── adr/                          ← system-wide decisions
├── src/
│   ├── ordering/
│   │   ├── CONTEXT.md
│   │   └── docs/adr/                 ← context-specific decisions
│   └── billing/
│       ├── CONTEXT.md
│       └── docs/adr/
```

Create files lazily: only when you have something to write. If no `CONTEXT.md` exists, create one when the first term is resolved. If no `docs/adr/` exists, create it when the first ADR is needed.

## During the session

### Challenge against the glossary

When the user uses a term that conflicts with the existing language in `CONTEXT.md`, call it out immediately. "Your glossary defines 'cancellation' as X, but you seem to mean Y. Which is it?"

### Sharpen fuzzy language

When the user uses vague or overloaded terms, propose a precise canonical term. "You're saying 'account': do you mean the Customer or the User? Those are different things."

### Discuss concrete scenarios

When domain relationships are being discussed, stress-test them with specific scenarios. Invent scenarios that probe edge cases and force the user to be precise about the boundaries between concepts.

### Cross-reference with code

When the user states how something works, check whether the code agrees. If you find a contradiction, surface it: "Your code cancels entire Orders, but you just said partial cancellation is possible. Which is right?"

### Update CONTEXT.md inline

When a term is resolved, update `CONTEXT.md` right there. Don't batch these up: capture them as they happen. Use the format in [CONTEXT-FORMAT.md](./CONTEXT-FORMAT.md).

`CONTEXT.md` should be totally devoid of implementation details. Do not treat `CONTEXT.md` as a spec, a scratch pad, or a repository for implementation decisions. It is a glossary and nothing else.

### Offer ADRs sparingly

Only offer to create an ADR when all three are true:

1. **Hard to reverse**: the cost of changing your mind later is meaningful
2. **Surprising without context**: a future reader will wonder "why did they do it this way?"
3. **The result of a real trade-off**: there were genuine alternatives and you picked one for specific reasons

If any of the three is missing, skip the ADR. Use the format in [ADR-FORMAT.md](./ADR-FORMAT.md).

Tool result:
# Context Map

The repo has more than one bounded context, each with its own language. This map points to where each glossary lives.

## Contexts

- [Public surface](./src/pages/CONTEXT.md) — the unauthenticated landing + sign-in page (`Auth.tsx`): how LSAT U describes itself to a prospective student.
- [Teacher workspace](./src/pages/teacher/CONTEXT.md) — Joshua's admin operator surface (`/teacher`): assigning prepared material to students and the transcript pipeline.
- [Teacher Desk page](./src/pages/teacher/desk/CONTEXT.md) — the named visual regions of the desk page itself (the boxes on screen), nested under the teacher workspace. Names the layout so we can revise it precisely.
- [Guided Drills & Explanations](./src/components/drill/CONTEXT.md) — the practice domain: guided drills, the question bank, drill instruction cards, and per-question explanations (the Phase 2 authoring work).
- [Classroom](./src/components/classroom/CONTEXT.md) — the student's own room: the Classroom frame, its sections, and the flag-versus-wrong-answer distinction.
- [Reading Comprehension](./src/features/rc/CONTEXT.md) — full-passage RC drilling: passages, sessions, attempts, reveal modes, status, and the adaptive picker.
- [Activity Centre](./src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md) — coach-led skill exercises a student is assigned, and the Read, Recall, Colour drill that runs inside one: Chunk, Detail, Coverage, The Recall, Report Card.
- [Booking](./src/components/booking/CONTEXT.md) — paying for and scheduling tutoring time: Packages, Booking Passes, the public microsite, and the Single Session (Slots, Holds, Bookers).

## Relationships

- Both surfaces describe the same product but to different audiences (prospective student vs. operator), so a word can carry different weight in each. Check the right glossary before reusing a term.
- A **flag** (Classroom) and a **wrong answer** (Wrong Answer Journal) are different things on different tables: a flag is chosen mid-question, a wrong answer is earned by missing one. Never collapse the two, and never let "flagged" drift into meaning "struggled with".
- **Set** now means one thing everywhere: the group a student assembles and drills. It used to mean a passage's position within its section in Reading Comprehension; that sense is now called **Position** (the data still carries `setNumber`). The collision was retired when the LR and RC pickers merged into one surface — a primary noun that changed meaning when you flipped the mode was the confusion the merge existed to remove. See `docs/adr/0006-rc-selection-is-question-grain.md`.

## assistant

I couldn't load `grilling`: it's set so only you can start it. If you want the full interview, type `/grilling`. `domain-modeling` did load, so I'm running the glossary side now against the drill glossary, [src/components/drill/CONTEXT.md](src/components/drill/CONTEXT.md).

I compared the coverage report I just made with that glossary. Four things don't line up:

1. **The glossary's count is out of date.** It says Question Explanations exist for "0 of 3,964" questions. Today about 3,119 have a full one, 298 have only Choice Notes, and 548 have nothing.
2. **My status labels aren't glossary terms.** In the report I called them "Missing", "Partial" and "Never loaded". The glossary already splits a Question Explanation into an **Argument Breakdown** and **Choice Notes**. So "Partial" really means *Choice Notes with no Argument Breakdown*.
3. **The reasoning-type names don't match the data.** The glossary says *Conditional Logic* and *Comparisons*, but the bank stores *Conditional Reasoning* and *Comparative Reasoning*.
4. **The number of question types doesn't match.** The glossary calls for about 25 canonical types. My report grouped them into 16.

I'll go one question at a time, starting with 2, because the other three depend on it.

**Question 1:** When a question has Choice Notes but no Argument Breakdown, does it count as *having* a Question Explanation?

My recommendation is **no**. The glossary says a Question Explanation is both halves, so one half alone is incomplete. I'd name the coverage states like this:
- **Complete:** both halves are there.
- **Notes-only:** Choice Notes, but no Argument Breakdown.
- **Unexplained:** neither half.
- **Unloaded:** the question isn't in the explanation tables at all.

That makes the headline figure 3,119 of 3,965 questions with an explanation, rather than 3,417.

Do you agree? And is **Notes-only** the word you'd use, or would you call it something else?

# Grill with docs

Read and apply both sibling files directly. Do not depend on invoking them through the Skill tool: a dependency may disable model invocation.

- [grilling](../grilling/SKILL.md): ask the current round of unresolved design questions.
- [domain-modeling](../domain-modeling/SKILL.md): maintain terminology and record decisions where appropriate.

Resolve file paths relative to this file. Keep both sibling skill folders, including domain-modeling's format references, alongside this skill when activating it.

Start from the conversation's settled requirements and the existing design document. Distinguish user decisions, assistant recommendations, and open questions. A correction overrides an earlier recommendation; document that change and do not re-ask settled questions. Look up facts rather than asking the user to supply them.

Ask only decisions whose prerequisites are settled, with a concrete example and recommendation where useful. When a user corrects an interaction, show a small example of that interaction before proposing further distinctions. Do not invent product differences just to justify previously proposed names or categories.

Update the existing design record as answers arrive. Update the owning glossary when a term is resolved; use the sibling domain-modeling templates. Create an ADR only when its trade-off criteria apply. Preserve source evidence and distinguish a drafted design from implemented behavior.

Recording settled answers and updating the design documents are part of the interview and happen as answers arrive. The grilling instruction to wait for shared understanding applies to implementing or publishing the proposed design, not to these interview records. Preserve authorization already given by the user.

# Grill with docs

Read and apply both sibling files directly. Do not depend on invoking them through the Skill tool: a dependency may disable model invocation.

- [grilling](../grilling/SKILL.md): ask the current round of unresolved design questions.
- [domain-modeling](../domain-modeling/SKILL.md): maintain terminology and record decisions where appropriate.

Resolve file paths relative to this file. Keep both sibling skill folders, including domain-modeling's format references, alongside this skill when activating it.

Start from the conversation's settled requirements and the existing design document. Distinguish user decisions, assistant recommendations, and open questions. A correction overrides an earlier recommendation; document that change and do not re-ask settled questions. Look up facts rather than asking the user to supply them.

Ask only decisions whose prerequisites are settled, with a concrete example and recommendation where useful. When a user corrects an interaction, show a small example of that interaction before proposing further distinctions. Do not invent product differences just to justify previously proposed names or categories.

Update the existing design record as answers arrive. Update the owning glossary when a term is resolved; use the sibling domain-modeling templates. Create an ADR only when its trade-off criteria apply. Preserve source evidence and distinguish a drafted design from implemented behavior.

Recording settled answers and updating the design documents are part of the interview and happen as answers arrive. The grilling instruction to wait for shared understanding applies to implementing or publishing the proposed design, not to these interview records. Preserve authorization already given by the user.
same-grilling

---
name: grilling
description: Grill the user relentlessly about a plan, decision, or idea. Use when the user wants to stress-test their thinking, or uses any 'grill' trigger phrases.
---

Interview the user relentlessly until you reach a shared understanding. Map this as a **design tree**: every decision branches into the decisions that hang off it.

Work the tree in **rounds**. The **frontier** is every decision whose prerequisites are already settled: the questions you can ask _now_ without guessing at answers you haven't heard yet. Ask the whole frontier in one round: number each question and give your recommended answer. Then wait for the user's answers before the next round.

Format a round like so:

```
❓ **Q1** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

➡️ <your recommended answer>

---

❓ **Q2** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

## assistant

I checked GitHub (mattpocock/skills), and my first fix was aimed at the wrong file. The real cause is one line that was added on your machine.

**What was actually wrong:** in Matt Pocock's repo, `grilling` can be called by `grill-with-docs`. I checked all 13 versions of the file in its history, and none has the setting that blocks that (`disable-model-invocation: true`). Your copies had it, and it's the only difference from upstream. Upstream's `grill-with-docs` calls `grilling` through the Skill tool, so that one line broke it.

**What I changed:**
- **Removed that line from all six copies of `grilling`**: the global one, `.claude`, `.agents`, and the catalog copies in `skills/claude`, `skills/codex` and `skills/gemini`. Each now matches upstream exactly.
- **Put the global `grill-with-docs` back to upstream.** This undoes my first fix, which is no longer needed.
- **Left the vault's own `grill-with-docs` as it is.** It reads the two skills directly instead of calling them, and it still works.
- **Updated `wiki/log.md`** with a correction under my earlier entry.

**Not verified yet:** whether `/grill-with-docs` runs end to end. It only picks up the change in a new session, so start a fresh one and run it there.

The glossary question is still open: should a question with Choice Notes but no Argument Breakdown count as having a Question Explanation?

{"file_path": "C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os\\system\\scripts\\sync-mattpocock-skills.py", "content": "\"\"\"Keep every installed mattpocock/skills copy in step with GitHub.\n\nWhich skills: every entry in ~/.agents/.skill-lock.json whose source is\nmattpocock/skills (installed by the `skills` CLI).\n\nWhere copies live on this machine (plain copies, not links):\n  ~/.agents/skills/<name>, ~/.claude/skills/<name>, and in the vault\n  .claude/skills, .agents/skills, skills/{claude,codex,gemini}/<name>.\n\nCompare-and-swap: a copy is replaced only when it still matches the upstream\nversion we last installed (recorded in the state file) or already matches the\nnew one. A copy that was edited locally is left alone and reported, so a\ndeliberate adaptation (e.g. the vault's grill-with-docs) is never overwritten.\n\nUsage: python sync-mattpocock-skills.py [--dry-run] [--adopt]\n  --adopt  first run only: treat copies whose SKILL.md matches upstream except\n           for frontmatter as clean. Without it, unknown copies are skipped.\n\"\"\"\nimport hashlib, io, json, os, shutil, subprocess, sys, tarfile, datetime, pathlib\n\nHOME = pathlib.Path.home()\nVAULT = pathlib.Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2]\nLOCK = HOME / \".agents\" / \".skill-lock.json\"\nSTATE = HOME / \".agents\" / \"mattpocock-sync-state.json\"\nLOG = VAULT / \"system\" / \"logs\" / \"mattpocock-skill-sync.log\"\nREPO = \"mattpocock/skills\"\nROOTS = [HOME / \".agents/skills\", HOME / \".claude/skills\", VAULT / \".claude/skills\",\n         VAULT / \".agents/skills\", VAULT / \"skills/claude\", VAULT / \"skills/codex\",\n         VAULT / \"skills/gemini\"]\nDRY = \"--dry-run\" in sys.argv\nADOPT = \"--adopt\" in sys.argv\n\n\ndef tree_hash(files):\n    h = hashlib.sha256()\n    for rel in sorted(files):\n        h.update(rel.encode()); h.update(b\"\\0\"); h.update(files[rel]); h.update(b\"\\0\")\n    return h.hexdigest()\n\n\ndef read_dir(d):\n    out = {}\n    for p in d.rglob(\"*\"):\n        if p.is_file() and \"__pycache__\" not in p.parts:\n            out[p.relative_to(d).as_posix()] = p.read_bytes().replace(b\"\\r\\n\", b\"\\n\")\n    return out\n\n\ndef fetch_upstream():\n    sha = subprocess.check_output([\"gh\", \"api\", f\"repos/{REPO}/commits/main\", \"--jq\", \".sha\"], text=True).strip()\n    raw = subprocess.check_output([\"gh\", \"api\", f\"repos/{REPO}/tarball/{sha}\"])\n    skills = {}\n    with tarfile.open(fileobj=io.BytesIO(raw)) as tf:\n        for m in tf.getmembers():\n            if not m.isfile():\n                continue\n            parts = m.name.split(\"/\")[1:]  # drop the tarball's top folder\n            if len(parts) >= 4 and parts[0] == \"skills\":\n                skill_dir = \"/\".join(parts[:3])  # skills/<group>/<name>\n                skills.setdefault(skill_dir, {})[\"/\".join(parts[3:])] = tf.extractfile(m).read()\n    return sha, skills\n\n\ndef main():\n    lock = json.loads(LOCK.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\"))\n    mine = {k: v for k, v in lock[\"skills\"].items() if v.get(\"source\") == REPO}\n    state = json.loads(STATE.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\")) if STATE.exists() else {\"skills\": {}}\n    sha, upstream = fetch_upstream()\n    lines, changed = [], False\n    for name, entry in sorted(mine.items()):\n        skill_dir = os.path.dirname(entry[\"skillPath\"].replace(\"\\\\\", \"/\"))\n        new = upstream.get(skill_dir)\n        if new is None:\n            lines.append(f\"GONE upstream: {name} ({skill_dir})\"); continue\n        new_h = tree_hash(new)\n        prev_h = state[\"skills\"].get(name)\n        for root in ROOTS:\n            d = root / name\n            if not d.is_dir() or d.is_symlink():\n                continue\n            cur = read_dir(d)\n            cur_h = tree_hash(cur)\n            if cur_h == new_h:\n                continue\n            clean = prev_h is not None and cur_h == prev_h\n            if not clean and ADOPT and \"SKILL.md\" in cur and \"SKILL.md\" in new:\n                body = lambda b: b.split(b\"\\n---\", 1)[-1]\n                clean = body(cur[\"SKILL.md\"]) == body(new[\"SKILL.md\"])\n            if not clean:\n                lines.append(f\"SKIP locally edited: {d}\"); continue\n            lines.append(f\"UPDATE {d}\")\n            if not DRY:\n                for rel in cur:\n                    if rel not in new:\n                        (d / rel).unlink()\n                for rel, data in new.items():\n                    (d / rel).parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n                    (d / rel).write_bytes(data)\n            changed = True\n        state[\"skills\"][name] = new_h\n        if not DRY:\n            entry[\"updatedAt\"] = datetime.datetime.utcnow().isoformat(timespec=\"milliseconds\") + \"Z\"\n    new_upstream = sorted(n.split(\"/\")[-1] for n in upstream\n                          if n.split(\"/\")[1] != \"deprecated\" and n.split(\"/\")[-1] not in mine)\n    if new_upstream:\n        lines.append(\"NOT INSTALLED (new upstream, not auto-added): \" + \", \".join(new_upstream))\n    stamp = datetime.datetime.now().strftime(\"%Y-%m-%d %H:%M\")\n    report = f\"[{stamp}] {REPO}@{sha[:7]} {'(dry run) ' if DRY else ''}{len(mine)} skills\\n\" + \"\".join(f\"  {l}\\n\" for l in lines)\n    print(report, end=\"\")\n    if not DRY:\n        state[\"commit\"] = sha\n        STATE.write_text(json.dumps(state, indent=2), encoding=\"utf-8\")\n        if changed:\n            LOCK.write_text(json.dumps(lock, indent=2), encoding=\"utf-8\")\n        LOG.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n        with LOG.open(\"a\", encoding=\"utf-8\") as f:\n            f.write(report)\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"}

---
> # Grill Me
> 
> One skill, self-contained. It holds both the **interview method** (Step 2) and the **capture discipline** (Steps 1 and 3): every answer lands in a durable markdown file, so nothing is lost as context fills up. It does not call the vendor `grilling` skill; that skill is `disable-model-invocation` and cannot be invoked from here. Its method is written inline below instead.
> 
> If the topic is a feature, component, or other implementation work rather than an open-ended plan or decision, `brainstorming` runs a similar interview but gates on a written, approved design doc before any code — reach for that instead when the destination is implementation.
> 
> ## Step 1: set up the capture file (BEFORE the first question)

< - **Automated checks**: are there automated checks that could catch errors the agent made? Linting, typing, tests, filesystem linters? Read the repo's own check command first (its `package.json`/build-tool `lint`/`check` scripts, its CI workflow), so a check that already exists but sits unwired or silently broken is the finding, not a reinvention. A repo with no **guardrail** (no pre-commit hook and no CI job running its lint/typecheck/test command) is itself a finding: an un-linted repo is a standing missed opportunity, not a neutral default. _Use when_ the agent made a mistake an automated check could have caught, or the repo has no guardrail at all.
< - **Coding standards**: should the **reviewer agent** be given a new rule to enforce? Should an existing rule be removed or clarified? Classify the violation first: a **mechanical** one (a fixed syntactic pattern, a banned API, an import shape, a file-location rule) gets a deterministic check, full stop: a custom rule in the repo's own linter, a new pre-commit hook, or a new CI job, whichever the repo's language and existing guardrail make cheapest. Default to building the check over writing the rule. Reserve `CODING_STANDARDS.md` for genuine **judgement calls** (cross-file consistency, "matches the surrounding style," anything no guardrail could ever substitute for). _Use when_ the reviewer agent failed to catch a mistake.

## user

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\Joshu\.claude\skills\grill-me

# Grill Me

One skill, self-contained. It holds both the **interview method** (Step 2) and the **capture discipline** (Steps 1 and 3): every answer lands in a durable markdown file, so nothing is lost as context fills up. It does not call the vendor `grilling` skill: the method is written inline below so this skill can add the capture steps around it and keep working even if `grilling` changes upstream.

If the topic is a feature, component, or other implementation work rather than an open-ended plan or decision, `brainstorming` runs a similar interview but gates on a written, approved design doc before any code — reach for that instead when the destination is implementation.

## Step 1: set up the capture file (BEFORE the first question)

1. Read the applicable `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`, then only the context pages and prior captures relevant to the topic. Starter-kit context lives in `context/`; in an established project follow its declared routes instead of creating a duplicate knowledge store. Reuse facts already supplied; ask about gaps, changes, decisions, and tradeoffs. Installing the skill does not start an interview.
2. Create the capture file at `brainstorms/{YYYY-MM-DD}-{topic-slug}.md` (create `brainstorms/` if missing). Every capture lives here, one predictable home regardless of topic. Do NOT scatter captures into project folders. A polished deliverable (plan, map, spec) may later move into the relevant `projects/` folder; the raw capture always stays in `brainstorms/`.
   - Get today's real date from the clock or shell (`Get-Date -Format yyyy-MM-dd` in PowerShell, `date +%F` in Bash).
   - Never overwrite an existing capture. Same date and topic: add a time or unique suffix and create exclusively. Resume an existing capture only when the user asks or clearly refers to that session; read it first and continue its question numbering.
   - No topic supplied or inferable: create an `untitled-discovery` capture with goal pending, ask what to explore, record the chosen topic and goal in that same file.
3. Write the header immediately: title, date, goal of the session, empty "Open flags" section.
4. Tell the user where you are saving, in one line.

## Step 2: run the interview

Interview the user relentlessly until you reach a shared understanding. Map this as a **design tree**: every decision branches into the decisions that hang off it.

Work the tree in **rounds**. The **frontier** is every decision whose prerequisites are already settled: the questions you can ask _now_ without guessing at answers you have not heard yet. Ask the whole frontier in one round: number each question and give your recommended answer. Then wait for the user's answers before the next round.

Format a round like so:

```
**Q1 - <question title>**: <question body, may be multiple paragraphs, including choices>

Recommended: <your recommended answer>

---

**Q2 - <question title>**: ...

Recommended: ...
```

Each round the user answers reshapes the tree: settled decisions push the frontier outward and unblock questions that depended on them. Recompute the frontier and ask the next round. A question whose answer depends on another question still open in this round belongs to a _later_ round, not this one.

Finding _facts_ is your job, never the user's. When a frontier question needs a fact from the environment (filesystem, tools, live site), look it up or dispatch a sub-agent; never ask the user for anything you could find yourself. Do not block on it: a running exploration is an unsettled prerequisite, so only the questions downstream of it wait; ask the rest of the frontier now. The _decisions_ are the user's: put each to them and wait.

The session is done when the frontier is empty: every branch visited, nothing silently assumed. Do not act on it until the user confirms you have reached a shared understanding.

Two adjustments the capture layer imposes on the method:

- For **decisions**, give a recommended answer grounded in context and label it as a recommendation. For **personal or business facts**, ask neutrally. Never lead with invented facts, and never save your inference as the user's answer.
- When the user **cannot answer** something, capture it as a flag with the right owner and move on. Do not stall the round on it.

## Step 3: the checkpoint rule (non-negotiable)

After EVERY round of answers, BEFORE you ask the next round:

- Append one structured entry per answered question: the question, the key facts and decisions from the answer (in the user's words where wording matters), and any flags (things they could not answer, plus who should).
- Update the running summary when a later answer changes it. Preserve the original entry, mark it superseded, and link to the correcting answer so the reasoning stays traceable.
- Read back the saved entries before continuing. If the write fails, say so, keep the answers visible in chat, and do not claim they were saved or collect more answers without a working checkpoint.
- Only then ask the next round.

Never let two rounds pile up in your head before writing. The file, not your context, is the source of truth. Never make the user ask you to save progress.

Keep user-confirmed facts, tentative ideas, your recommendations, and unresolved questions distinguishable in the capture.

## Capture file structure

```
# {Topic}: Brainstorm / Discovery Notes
Date: {date} · Goal: {one line}
Status: in progress / paused / complete
Context sources: {relevant existing pages, if any}

## Summary / key decisions
(running synthesis, updated as you go)

## Q&A log
### Round 1
#### Q1 - {topic}
- Asked: {question}
- Recommended: {your recommendation, labeled}
- Captured: {user-confirmed facts and decisions, in their words where it matters}
- Tentative: {unconfirmed ideas, clearly labeled}
- Flags: {open item -> owner}
#### Q2 - ...
### Round 2
...

## Open flags (pending input)
- {item} -> {who can answer}
```

## At the end or on pause

Respect an explicit stop immediately; do not add a new question. Near the end, offer a completeness backstop ("anything we haven't touched?"). Then:

- Read the capture for contradictions or gaps. Mark unresolved conflicts explicitly; do not choose a business fact on the user's behalf. Update status and resume point.
- For sessions explicitly about building or updating AI OS context, merge user-confirmed durable facts and preferences into the appropriate existing context page, with a dated link back to the capture. Preserve unrelated content. Record confirmed meaningful decisions in the existing decision log without duplicating entries. Unknowns, brainstorm ideas, and your recommendations stay labeled in the capture until confirmed.
- For a plan or design interview without a request to update canonical context, the raw capture is the output. Suggest `/link` for routing a useful result, or `/level-up` for turning a selected opportunity into one improvement. Do not run either automatically.
- Ensure the operating manual or an existing index routes to `brainstorms/` when capture routing is absent, using the project's conventions and preserving shared manual parity. Individual captures do not each need a root-manual entry. Raw captures are dated interview evidence, not automatically current business truth.
- Give a short recap: clickable capture path, context pages actually updated, remaining flags, next step or resume point.

Do not act on the plan until the user confirms you have reached a shared understanding.

## Boundaries and verification

- Scope is the local interview capture and any requested context updates. No global-memory edits, publication, messages, or external-system changes. Do not request or save credentials; use references or redactions for secrets.
- The starter kit ignores `brainstorms/` in Git by default. This does not remove captures already tracked in an existing repository.
- Validate a fresh session, a same-day name collision, a resume, a correcting answer, a stop request, and a failed write. Verify that only confirmed facts reach canonical context and that earlier answers remain traceable.

- **Unexplained:** neither half.
- **Unloaded:** the question isn't in the explanation tables at all.

//
//   node scripts/knowledge/ingest-lr-explanations.mjs <dir-of-v2-md-files>
//
// Sources:

// Every file now uses the standard layout; the legacy "## Question N" parser
// is no longer reachable from here.
//
// Strategy: parse everything in memory, COPY into temp staging tables, then
// UPSERT into the real tables in one transaction. Idempotent: re-running

// Two write paths, same staging + upsert SQL:

//                     Conflict keys are (preptest, section, question_number)
//                     for questions, (question_id, letter) for choices,
//                     (question_id, style) for explanations, and steps/notes

**Q5 - The glossary says explanations are never generated**: The glossary defines a Question Explanation as "transcribed LSAT Lab content, never generated." Explanations Claude writes would break that rule. Should the database record who wrote each one, and should students ever see it?

Recommended: Record the writer on each explanation (LSAT Lab or LSAT U) and don't show it to students. Update the glossary to say an explanation can come from either source. The record lets you find, review or replace Claude's explanations later without guessing which ones they are.

---

**Q6 - Review before students see it**: Do you review Claude's explanations before they go live?

Recommended: Start with a pilot of about 20 questions across the four biggest gap types: Must Be True / Most Supported, Flaw, Necessary Assumption and Paradox. You review every one of those. Once the pilot passes, you spot-check about 1 in 10 per batch. Don't publish anything unreviewed until the pilot is approved.

---

**Q7 - Which styles to write**: LSAT Lab published up to three versions of each explanation: Standard, Brief and Another Way. Which does Claude write for the missing questions?

Recommended: Standard only, with Choice Notes on every answer. Standard is the full walkthrough, and each question has one. Brief and Another Way are extras LSAT Lab added only on some questions. Adding them for all 846 triples the writing for little benefit.

---

**Q8 - Questions that already have Choice Notes (298)**: For the Notes-only questions, does Claude keep LSAT Lab's existing Choice Notes and add only the missing Argument Breakdown?

Recommended: Yes. Keep LSAT Lab's notes as they are and write only the Argument Breakdown, making sure it agrees with those notes. That way nothing already transcribed gets overwritten.

---

**Q9 - Order after the first load**: Once PT154, PT156 and PT159 are loaded, which gaps get written first?

Recommended: Questions students are actually assigned come first: guided-drill questions and assigned homework sections. Then the rest, grouped by question type, so each batch draws on the same examples and stays consistent.

Five decisions are still open. I've shortened the ones you didn't get to. A one-word answer each is enough.

**Q5 - Record who wrote it**: Should each explanation be marked in the database as LSAT Lab or LSAT U, with the mark hidden from students?

Recommended: Yes. Without it, you can't later tell Claude's explanations apart from LSAT Lab's.

---

**Q6 - Review**: Should Claude start with a pilot of about 20 explanations that you review in full, then continue with spot checks?

Recommended: Yes, and nothing goes live before the pilot is approved.

---

Recommended: Standard only.

---

**Q8 - The 298 Notes-only questions**: These have LSAT Lab's answer-choice notes but no argument walkthrough. Do they count as missing, so Claude writes just the walkthrough and leaves the notes alone?

Recommended: Yes. Without it they're half-explained, and adding it keeps every existing note as it is.

---

**Q9 - Order**: Should Claude start with questions students are actually assigned, then work through the rest by question type?

Recommended: Yes.

---

**Q10 - How the golden set gets picked**: Should Claude choose the 10 to 30 examples, and should you approve them before any writing starts?

Recommended: Claude proposes about 25, covering the main question types, ranked for clear structure, a complete walkthrough, and notes on all five answers. You approve or swap them. Everything that follows copies whatever is in this set, so you should see it first.

---

**Q11 - How much can Claude improve on the originals**: You want the new explanations to match the originals and also be easier to follow. Where is Claude allowed to depart from them?

Recommended: Keep the originals' structure, their step labels (Conclusion, Evidence, Evaluate and so on), and their tone and teaching approach. Improve only clarity: shorter sentences, and one idea per step. Explain every step in full, with no gaps in the reasoning. Never add a new format or new labels, so a student can't tell which explanations are new.

Four decisions left:

**Q9 - Order of work** (you said assigned questions first isn't necessarily the order): What order should Claude write in?

Recommended: Work by question type, in batches. Do all the missing Flaw questions together, then Necessary Assumption, and so on. A batch shares the same golden examples and references, so it comes out consistent, and you can review a whole type at once. Start with the pilot types: Must Be True / Most Supported, Flaw, Necessary Assumption and Paradox.

---

**Q12 - A written framework**: Should the "strong framework" be a written guide? It would be taken from the golden set and cover each question type: what the walkthrough covers, what the answer-choice notes do, and where Claude may adapt. You'd approve it together with the golden set.

Recommended: Yes, one guide in the vault. Otherwise the framework only exists in my head for one session. With a guide, a later session or Codex writing batch 12 follows the same rules as batch 1, which is what keeps all 846 consistent.

---

**Q13 - When Claude disagrees with the correct answer on file**: Sometimes the bank's correct answer may look wrong, or be mis-keyed. What should Claude do?

Recommended: Never write an explanation that argues for an answer it doesn't believe. Flag the question with its reasoning, skip it, and list it for you to decide. A confident explanation of a wrong answer does more harm than a missing one.

---

**Q14 - Where drafts live before going live**: Should Claude's explanations be written in the same markdown format as the transcribed LSAT Lab files, and loaded through the same script that loads those?

Recommended: Yes. Put them in a separate folder so they never mix with the transcribed files, and load them with the same script, which records that LSAT U wrote them. You review readable files before anything touches the database. The student view needs no new code, because it already displays this format.

const srcDir = process.argv[2];
if (!srcDir) {
  console.error('usage: ingest-lr-explanations.mjs <dir-of-v2-md-files>');
  process.exit(1);
}
const repoExplanations = path.resolve('Explanations');

const questions = [];
const skipped = [];
const sources = [];

const ingestStandard = (file, label) => {
  const r = parseExplanationFile(fs.readFileSync(file, 'utf8'), path.basename(file));
  questions.push(...r.questions);
  skipped.push(...r.skipped.map((s) => ({ ...s, file: label })));
  sources.push({ file: label, parsed: r.questions.length, skipped: r.skipped.length });
};

// --- batch 1: the v2 zip (PT101-PT140) ---
for (const f of fs.readdirSync(srcDir).filter((f) => /^PT\d+-explanations\.md$/.test(f)).sort()) {
  ingestStandard(path.join(srcDir, f), `v2/${f}`);
}

// --- batch 2: in-repo PT141-PT159 ---
if (fs.existsSync(repoExplanations)) {
  const repoFiles = fs
    .readdirSync(repoExplanations)
    .filter((f) => /^PT\d+-explanations\.md$/.test(f))
    .filter((f) => {
      const pt = Number(/^PT(\d+)/.exec(f)[1]);
      return pt >= 141 && pt <= 159; // PT139/PT140 here are stale duplicates
    })
    .sort();
  for (const f of repoFiles) ingestStandard(path.join(repoExplanations, f), `repo/${f}`);

  // PT155 section 2 exists nowhere else. It was reformatted from the older
  // "## Question N" layout into the standard one on 2026-08-03, which also

  const s2 = path.join(repoExplanations, 'PT155-S2-explanations.md');
  if (fs.existsSync(s2)) ingestStandard(s2, 'repo/PT155-S2-explanations.md');
}

// Guard against a duplicate (preptest, section, question_number) across files.
const seen = new Map();
const deduped = [];
for (const q of questions) {
  if (seen.has(q.qid)) {
    skipped.push({ id: q.qid, reason: `duplicate question id (already parsed from another file)` });
    continue;
  }
  seen.set(q.qid, true);
  deduped.push(q);
}

// ---------- build TSV payloads ----------
const esc = (v) => {
  if (v === null || v === undefined) return '\\N';
  return String(v)
    .replace(/\\/g, '\\\\')
    .replace(/\n/g, '\\n')
    .replace(/\r/g, '\\r')
    .replace(/\t/g, '\\t');
};
const row = (...cells) => cells.map(esc).join('\t');

// A SQL string literal. standard_conforming_strings is on, so backslashes are
// literal and doubling the single quote is the whole escape. NULs cannot live
// in a Postgres text value at all, so they are dropped rather than smuggled in.
const lit = (v) => {
  if (v === null || v === undefined) return 'NULL';
  if (typeof v === 'boolean') return v ? 'true' : 'false';
  if (typeof v === 'number') return Number.isFinite(v) ? String(v) : 'NULL';
  return `'${String(v).replace(/\0/g, '').replace(/'/g, "''")}'`;
};
const tuple = (...cells) => `(${cells.map(lit).join(', ')})`;

const qRows = [];
const cRows = [];
const eRows = [];
const sRows = [];
const nRows = [];

// The same five payloads again, grouped per question and rendered as SQL tuples,
// so the emit path can cut chunks without ever splitting a question's rows
// across two transactions.
const groups = [];

## S1 · Q1 — Agree/Disagree

**Correct answer:** B

> Note: LSAT Lab offers only a single authored written explanation for this question (no Standard / Brief / Another Way selector).

### Stimulus

Rhett: Otto gives me a ride to work every morning and expects me to help pay for fuel. But I shouldn't have to pay. Giving me a ride doesn't increase the amount he spends on fuel, since he has to go right by my house anyway.

Barbara: By that flawed logic, you would be entitled to the warm air that heats the unused spare room of my house, if you could divert it to your house without increasing my expenses.

### Prompt

The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Rhett and Barbara disagree over whether

### Written (single style)

**Argument breakdown**

*Rhett's Claims*
1. Otto gives me a ride to work every morning.
2. Otto expects me to help pay for fuel.
3. I shouldn't have to pay.
4. Giving me a ride doesn't increase the amount he spends on fuel.
5. He has to go right by my house anyway.

*Barbara's Responses*
1. no comment
2. no comment
3. Disagrees
4. no comment (possibly agrees)
5. no comment

*Evaluate:* Barbara doesn't dispute any of the facts that Rhett presents as evidence, such as Rhett's claim #1, #2, or #5. She might even agree withe claim #4, because she mentions diverting warm air from her house "without increasing my expenses." So, when Barbara states that Rhett's logic is "flawed," she seems to be disagreeing with his conclusion that he should not have have to pay Otto.

*Goal:* We'll look for an answer that addresses claim #3.

**Answer choices**
**A. Otto requires Rhett to help pay for fuel for the ride to work** — Too Strong (3% of students)

  }));

function parseBreakdown(lines) {
  const steps = [];
  let inBreakdown = false;
  for (const raw of lines) {
    const line = raw.trimEnd();
    if (/^\*\*Argument breakdown\*\*\s*$/.test(line)) {
      inBreakdown = true;
      continue;
    }
    if (/^\*\*Answer choices\*\*\s*$/.test(line)) break;
    const m = STEP_RE.exec(line);
    if (m && (inBreakdown || true)) {
      inBreakdown = true;
      steps.push({ label: m[1].trim(), text: m[2].trim() });
      continue;
    }
    // Alternate authored shape: the label is its own bold line, with the step
    // text in the paragraph(s) below it (e.g. "**Given that ...**").
    const h = /^\*\*(.+?)\*\*\s*$/.exec(line);
    if (h) {
      inBreakdown = true;
      steps.push({ label: h[1].replace(/\s*\.\.\.\s*$/, '').trim(), text: '' });
      continue;
    }
    if (!inBreakdown) continue;
    if (line.trim() && steps.length) {
      const prev = steps[steps.length - 1];
      prev.text = prev.text ? `${prev.text}\n${line}` : line;
    }
  }
  return steps.filter((s) => s.text.trim());
}

### Stimulus

Horses, although descended from a forest-dwelling ancestor, thrive in grasslands. In the last 2 million years, horses have gone through three cycles of population increase followed by a rapid decline in population. The most recent cycle peaked 25,000 years ago during a cold period that preceded a period of warming.

### Prompt

Which one of the following, if true, most helps to explain why the horse population peaked 25,000 years ago and then rapidly declined?

### Written (single style)

======

### Stimulus

Editorial: Some primary schools' early education reading programs encourage children to read by paying them for each book they read outside of class. Such programs should be viewed with suspicion. While paying kids to read might get them to read more, it also might teach them to regard reading as a chore rather than as a source of intrinsic satisfaction.

### Prompt

Which one of the following principles, if valid, most strongly supports the editorial's reasoning?

6	('PT154-S2-Q1', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'It''s fine to consider the first sentence as part of the evidence, but it''s really giving us an idea of how high the average CEO''s pay is when compared to the average worker. If anything, we might bake this information into the conclusion:
7	It is wholly unjustified to pay CEOs many times more than the average worker at their company is paid.
8	In order for the evidence to fully support this conclusion, we need to assume that, if a CEO does not work many times harder than the average worker at their company, it is wholly unjustified to pay the CEO many times more than the average worker is paid.
9	We could even diagram this:
10	~work many times harder → ~pay many times higher'),
11	('PT154-S2-Q1', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since this is a Principle-Strengthen question, the correct answer might contain this exact assumption. However, the correct answer only needs to strengthen the argument. A principle which states something like, "a person''s pay should be directly related to how hard they work" would be good enough. Beware of answers that contain illegal reversals or illegal negations of the assumption we identified above.'),
12	('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'In standing against regimentation and standardization of any sort, Cummings stood against something essential to the work he did (standing for the individual human being).'),
13	('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since / and)', 'Metaphor presupposes literal language,
14	and
15	literal language essentially involves regimentation.'),
16	('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Excuse me, waiter? Hi, I believe there''s a mistake. I ordered a question 2, but this seems way too hard to read for the 2nd question in an LR section.
17	Okay, when we''re confused we can lean on structural clues. We have to find out Conclusion, and "since" and "and" instruct our eyes that the final two claims are supporting ideas.
18	The conclusion has a pronoun part of it, "in doing so", so we need to import language from the first sentence in order to grasp the full claim that the Conclusion is making.
19	When we''re confused on Sufficient Assumption, we can lean on a trick I call "Mentioned twice? That''ll suffice." The basic idea is that ideas that are mentioned twice (literal language, regimentation, stood against) are pretty much taken care of, in terms of the internal logic of an argument.
20	Consider this "argument":
21	If A then B. Thus, if A then D, since B presupposes C.
22	Without thinking too hard, we can guess that the missing link is between C and D, since they are only mentioned once, whereas A and B are each mentioned twice.
23	So in this Cummings argument, the stuff only mentioned once is "essential to the work he did" and "metaphor".
24	To actually understand and predict that link, we should first take advantage of the mini-chain of ideas the Premises are giving us in the final two claims.
25	Metaphor presupposes literal language. Literal language involves regimentation.
26	Metaphor → literal language → regimentation
27	If you''re against regimentation, as they say Cummings was, then by contrapositive, you would be against literal language and against metaphor.
28	The author is saying, "If Cummings stood against metaphor, then Cummings stood against something essential to the work he did."'),
29	('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Thus, we want something like "metaphor was essential to the work Cummings did".'),
30	('PT154-S2-Q6', 'single', 0, 'Given', 'Video games are commonly criticized as morally corrupting in that they allow people to imagine themselves transgressing conventional morality.

32	It now seems silly to suggest that reading novels is morally corrupting'),
33	('PT154-S2-Q6', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'This argument doesn''t contain a clear conclusion indicator word like "thus," or even a clear premise indicator like "since." However, it does seem to follow a common pattern.
34	The first sentence describes a common belief.
35	The next sentence begins with a pivot indicator, "but." The statements that follow the pivot indicator refute the common belief presented in the first sentence.
36	When we see the phrase "and just as" at the beginning of the very last sentence, it indicates that whatever goes in the blank should continue in this direction that the argument is headed: it should continue to refute the idea presented in the first sentence.
37	The argument compares the current criticism of video games to earlier criticism of novels, movies, and rock music, stating that these were also seen as morally corrupting. The argument claims that it now seems silly to criticize novels this way.
38	We would expect this argument to conclude that it seems equally silly to suggest that video games are morally corrupting.'),

51	('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 1, 'Application', 'Paper Flowers should not be removed.'),
52	('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'When we''re given principles, we want to always arrange them so that the Criterion is on the left and the Judgy term (should not be removed) is on the right.

54	for X to happen, Y and Z must be true
55	Which contraposes into
56	if Y false or Z false, X shouldn''t happen
57	The second rule said
58	Things that are Y or Z can be X only if Z
59	That one is too complex to have one standard rule to go off of. We''re better off thinking, "the default sentiment here is that This group of books can be removed only if they haven''t been checked out for over three years"
60	That look like
61	checked out within last 3 yrs → can''t be removed
62	Then we just added on to the left side what criteria makes you part of "this group" of books:
63	written by local author, significant to local history, or both
64	You could consider fudging the details there and just saying
65	```
66	Locally important
67	and → can''t be removed
68	checked out w/in last 3
69	```'),
70	('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Assuming we symbolized the rules properly, the hard part is over. Since the Application is saying that PF shouldn''t be removed, we can get there by establishing that PF either triggers the first policy or the second.
71	So we either need to hear that PF
72	1. isn''t badly damaged
73	2. has been checked out in the past 2 years
74	or
75	3. is "locally important" and has been checked out in the past 3 years
76	Any of those three conditions will do it.'),

83	('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It''s a myth that a significant amount of music online is the result of people downloading others'' music and reworking it into new music of their own. 84	('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', '99% of people who download music do not publish new music of their own on the internet.'),
85	('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'There are a lot of LSAT arguments testing the idea of "how could a small slice make a big impact?", and we''re basically supposed to say "maybe that small slice has a disproportionately large effect on the situation".

89	But, if this 1 person creates 50 songs that way, and the other 99 people don''t publish any new music of their own, then when it comes to those 100 people, the only music they''re contributing to the Internet is those 50 songs that were of the download/sample/publish variety.'),

Since this argument has a Sampling issue (the author is fighting a genera claim based on a just a couple of nonconfirming data points), let''s look for an argument relying on a sample that is too small.

111	('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It is a mistake to criticize the city for being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets.

113	The city''s not being overzealous (or if they are, we shouldn''t criticize them for it).'),
114	('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Can you imagine how much worse parking would be if parking regulations were not enforced?'),
115	('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This is a Straw Man argument, which is a term people use in real life to describe it, but LSAT has never used that wording to describe it. The idea of a Straw Man rebuttal is that you''re unfairly representing the position being debated.
116	If I say, "I think we should extend unemployment benefits another six months, while we recover from the pandemic" and you say, "Well I don''t think it''s the government''s job to make people rich for doing nothing!" ...
117	... you''re not arguing with my position; you''re fighting a Straw Man position you designed, because it makes for an easy opponent (a piñata you can knock down).
118	Extending unemployment benefits (which means paying a jobless person around $1200 / month, which is well below the poverty line) is not making people rich for doing nothing. That''s not an intellectually respectful rebuttal to the original position.
119	In this parking ticket argument, the original position in the critics'':
120	the city is being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets.
121	Think about what that means. Give yourself a mental picture, a couple examples. What does it mean for a meter maid to be issuing tickets overzealously?
122	Maybe the meter runs out for some car at 5:58pm, but the rules for that spot are that you only need to pay until 6pm, after which it''s free. Rather than letting it slide, the overzealous meter maid gives you a ticket for those 2 unpaid minutes.
123	Maybe your car is parked a little too far up, in terms of the parking spot lines or in terms of the red part of the curb where you can''t park. Rather than tolerate your front bumper being six inches over the red line, they overzealously give you a ticket for illegal parking.
124	The author''s method of arguing is saying, "You shouldn''t criticize the status quo, because the alternative is worse!" You shouldn''t criticize this obnoxiously overzealous ticketing by the meter maids, because the alternative, a wold in which no parking regulations are enforced, would be worse.
125	That''s like a parent handing their kid a bowl of lentil soup, and the kid saying, "Ouch, that''s too hot", and the parent responding, "You shouldn''t criticize. Can you imagine how much worse it would be if it were super cold, with ice cubes in it?"
126	The kid would properly respond, "Well, that seems like a False Choice. Why does it have to be scalding hot or unappetizingly cold? Isn''t there a middle ground?"'),
127	('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Let''s look for an answer making a similar complaint:
128	Hey, author, isn''t there a middle ground between overzealous ticketing and no ticketing at all? You can''t say the former is justified because the latter is worse, unless those are our only two options.'),
129	('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It is better that graphic designers not remain anonymous.'),
130	('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Anonymity undermines effective graphic design by making it difficult to hold designers accountable for their work.'),
131	('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'When we''re doing Main Conclusion and Role, we prime our brain with one big tendency before we start reading the paragraph: the vast majority of the time, the Conclusion will come before the Evidence.
132	On Main Conclusion, this manifests in two big patterns:
133	- the very first claim of the paragraph is the Main Conclusion
134	or
135	- the author does a little warm-up with Background or Counterpoint ideas, and then pivots into her Conclusion, usually with a "but, yet, however".
136	This argument followed that second pattern, introducing a Background / Counterpoint idea (graphic designers aren''t allowed to sign their works).
137	Then it pivots with a "nonetheless" into the Conclusion. Then it supports that Conclusion with Evidence.'),
138	('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'On Main Conclusion, if we found the conclusion, we already did the hard part. Picking a correct answer means that we''re just making ourselves pick whichever answer choice is closer in meaning to the claim we identified (and possibly highlighted) as the Main Conclusion.
139	So we want something that says, "It is better that graphic designers not remain anonymous".
140	Consider what an equivalent rephrasing might sound like, to make your listening ears more nimble: "it would be cool if graphic designers did sign their names" / "having graphic designers remain anonymous is overall not great".'),
141	('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (Clearly, then)', 'Participation in a work internship decreases the chance that a student will drop out.'),
142	('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Of the high school students who participate in work internships, only 1% drop out (compared to 11% of students overall).'),
143	('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author is looking at a curious fact (an asymmetric comparison) and then making a causal conclusion (X decreases the chance of Y).
144	Curious Fact ... how come the dropout rate for students in internships is way lower than it is for students overall?
145	Author''s Causal Explanation ... it must be that participating in the internship causes students to be less likely to drop out.
146	On Flaw (and Weaken and Evaluate and Necessary Assumption), we primarily react to these Explain Curious Fact arguments by thinking, "could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact?"
147	With correlations, we''re programmed like robots to think about potential alternate explanations like Reverse Cause / 3rd Factor:
148	- Reverse Cause: is it possible that not-dropping out is causing students to have the internship?
149	- 3rd Factor: could there be some underlying factor (like good grades) that is causing students to stay in school and causing them to be a part of an internship?'),
150	('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'With Flaw, we''re mainly trying to call out the fact that the author is overconfidently selling us on one possible causal explanation for a curious fact, without ruling out other possible explanations.
151	So look for an answer to object to the move from a correlation between internships and dropout rates to an assumption that the two are causally related.
152	Or look for an answer that discusses an alternate explanation for this association between internships and lower dropout rates.'),
153	('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (Thus)', 'It''s likely that the party now in power will retain power after the upcoming elections.'),
154	('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'Voter confidence in the economy tends to favor incumbent political leaders.'),
155	('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 2, 'Intermediate Conclusion (Clearly)', 'Investors are confident the economy will see increased growth.'),
156	('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 3, 'Evidence', 'Many investors are putting money into stocks.'),
157	('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 4, 'Evaluate', 'There''s a real keyword gumbo going on here: clearly, thus, since. These indicate multiple conclusions, so we theoretically have different reasoning moves to analyze, if we''re looking for assumptions.
158	The logic of all the indicator words works like this:
159	```
160	Despite
161	[counterpoint], opposing idea
162	claim 1. prem supporting 2
163	Clearly, claim 2. conc, supported by 1.
164	Thus, conc, supported by 2.
165	since claim 3, prem, supporting 4.
166	claim 4. conc, supp by 2+4
167	```
168	I typed that and it looks like crazytalk even to me, but each of those indicator words tells you a story of who''s supporting whom or opposed to whom. It''s not a replacement for comprehension, but it''s a way to understand things when you''re not comprehending very well. (sort of like relying on conditional logic indicator words to deal with conditional logic).
169	Our author''s two linking assumptions, therefore, would sound like this
170	```
171	If ... Then ...
172	investors are putting       investors are confident
173	money into stocks → in economic growth
174	If ... Then ...
175	investors are confident
176	in economic growth       The incumbents
177	and → will retain power
178	voter confidence tends
179	to favor incumbents
180	```
181	The first linking assumption was joining claim 1 to claim 2.
182	The second was joining claims 2 and 3 to claim 4.
183	More conversationally, this author is expecting the incumbents to win the upcoming election, because when voters are confident in the economy, incumbents usually win.
184	Okay .... are voters confident in the economy?
185	The author would say "Sure, why just look at how confident investors are right now!"
186	Okay ... does investor confidence match voter confidence?'),
187	('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 5, 'Goal', 'The correct answer could test the move from "putting money into stock market" to "confident in growth of economy", but it''s more likely to address the switch the argument makes from talking about investor confidence to talking about voter confidence, while acting like we''re still talking about the same thing.'),
188	('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The rubbish tells us very little about the possession of the people who lived nearby.'),
189	('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The pits have been subject to erosion over long periods of time, with destructive effects on the rubbish within.'),
190	('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This question stem is asking us to strengthen the grounds on which the conclusion is based, so it''s specifically asking us to improve the force / relevance of the evidence.
191	In order for us to figure out what the answer might sound like, we often benefit from thinking about how we''d object to the argument. If we adopt a Weaken mindset, we can get our brain to think of objections, and then those objections become ways to Strengthen the argument (if we rule out or go the opposite direction of those ideas).
192	given that erosion has had destructive effects on the rubbish within these pits,
193	how could someone still argue that the rubbish does tell us a lot about the possessions of the people who lived nearby?
194	I guess we''d just say, "sure it''s been damaged, but you can still figure out what most of it is ... or you can learn a lot about their potential possessions based on the types of chemical materials you see in there".'),

198	- nope, can''t learn anything important by doing a chemical analysis of the eroded trash.
199	- this was the worst kind of erosion'),
200	('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (shouldn''t = opinion)', 'The mayor shouldn''t adopt her rival''s controversial proposal for solving the city''s budget problem.'),
201	('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'If she adopts the proposal and it succeeds, she''ll potentially increase her rival''s credibility.

203	('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Structurally, it feels like the the argument is saying:

205	EVID: If X succeeds, something bad happens to us. If X fails, something bad happens to us.'),
206	('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for a conclusion saying "we shouldn''t do something", and premises (probably both conditional) that feel like "damned if we do, damned if we don''t".'),
207	('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'If it''s correct to define culture as the totality of customs practiced by those whose culture it is, then the customs of a culture necessarily function to preserve it, even if just in a trivial sense.'),
208	('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (because)', 'Under this definition of culture, if a culture were to abandon any custom, then by definition it would destroy that culture.'),
209	('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This author is making a weird, nerdy argument that will make nerdy logicians snap their fingers in nerdy approval.
210	Anthropologists are debating whether the customs in a culture help to preserve that culture. (i.e. does celebrating Thanksgiving help to preserve American culture?)
211	The author is not engaging with the substantive truth of whether customs are a way of reinforcing and solidifying cultural identity. Instead, the author is interested in just making an observation about what is technically true, in a vacuous sense.
212	Suppose you define a culture as "the unique set of customs practiced by that culture".
213	Culture X does customs A, B, and C.
214	Culture Y does customs A, B, and D.
215	Even though they have customs in common, the totality of ABC is different from the totality of ABD, so according to this definition they are each different cultures.
216	The author''s cute observation is that if Culture X were to drop custom C, then they would only do customs A and B. Thus, they would no longer be Culture X, since that culture was defined as the ABC set of customs.
217	A culture with an AB set of customs is different from a culture with an ABC set of customs. Thus, getting rid of custom C made the group that used to be Culture X no longer be Culture X.
218	Abandoning that custom destroyed their status as Culture X, so, technically, having custom C preserves Culture X.'),
219	('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We''re being asked about the 2nd sentence. This provides a definition of "culture" as "unique set of customs" so that the author can make her cute mathematical argument.
220	We could maybe call it a premise, because the author is definitely using it in tandem with the final claim in order to derive the conclusion. But it feels like we could also call it a background claim that the author just scoops up and says, "If this happened to be true, then inference X would be true because premise Y."
221	The answer will probably sound relational (not as simple as premise / conclusion / background / opposing) and convey the idea that the author is just saying, "by THIS definition, culture definitely preserves culture in a technical, mathematical sense".'),
222	('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (conclude / therefore)', 'The pressure on the nerve in question was causing this patient''s back/leg pain.'),
223	('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'A computer scan suggested there was pressure on a nerve going from the lower vertebra to the leg.
224	This type of pressure can cause inflammation, which can cause pain.
225	If the nerve was inflamed, cortisone would reduce the inflammation. We injected cortisone and then the patient experienced pain relief.'),
226	('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Since the doctor is trying to Solve the Causal Mystery of this patient''s nerve/back pain, we could use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset.
227	The doctor is trying to explain the curious back/leg pain and explains it via pressure on the nerve in question. So we would worry about Alternate Explanations for the back/leg pain and we would worry about the plausibility of the doctor''s diagnosis.
228	The most important moment to notice is when the doctor introduces a conditional claim:
229	if the nerve was inflamed, cortisone would help.
230	Cortisone helped. Thus, the nerve was inflamed.
231	What''s wrong with that mini-argument?
232	Well, that''s an illegal reversal of course. Just because cortisone helped doesn''t show that the doctor''s diagnosis was correct.
233	Perhaps there are other possible causes for which cortisone would also help. We haven''t ruled out any of those possibilities.'),
234	('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer saying something necessary to the internal logic of the doctor''s diagnosis, or look for an answer ruling out the possibility of an alternate diagnosis.'),
235	('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The public''s welfare can be undermined by its own tastes.'),
236	('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (for example ...)', 'Journalists, because they want to focus on stories that stimulate readers, are increasingly doing gossipy stuff rather than hard news.'),
237	('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Whenever we do Main Conclusion questions, we prime our brain to read the paragraph in that special way you read Main Conclusion and Role paragraphs —
238	the conclusion will probably be earlier than the support
239	Normally, in Logical Reasoning, the Conclusion is more likely to come at the end of the paragraph. But that tendency big time flips on Main Conclusion and Role. So you start listening as early as the very first sentence.
240	You have to ask yourself actively as you read each claim, "Is this the conclusion? Does it sound like the author''s opinion? Does the next sentence start off feeling like it''s trying to support this claim?"
241	On this question, the first sentence doesn''t quite sound like an opinion, but it does sound like an abstract claim, rather than a specific fact. Does the next sentence sound like it''s supporting this initial claim?
242	Honestly, no. But if we force ourselves to look closer, there is an overlapping element. They''re both talking about "the public''s tastes" / "the readers'' interest".
243	The first sentence was positing a causal connection:
244	public''s tastes --> undermines public welfare
245	This second sentence is talking about the public''s taste, when it comes to news stories. Does it say that our taste in news stories leads to "undermining public welfare"?
246	Yes, aloofly. It goes on to complain that serious news stories have been displaced by TMZ and InfoWars type stories (the author is assuming that this switch in emphasis is something that undermines public welfare, but we don''t need to worry about assumptions on Main Conclusion).
247	Incidentally the "Because" being used in the final sentence should probably be interpreted as a causal usage of Because (as in, "Because I fell down, my knee is bleeding"), not a reasoning usage of Because, as a support indicator.'),
248	('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Our only job is to find the Conclusion. We did. This paragraph made it particularly hard to do so. It''s the first sentence. It sure makes life easier if we know the trick for Main Conclusion questions that the conclusion is earlier than the support, the vast majority of the time.
249	Let''s find an answer that mimics the meaning of the first sentence.'),
250	('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It''s now undeniable that humans have an effect on the weather.'),
251	('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'A study of 30 urban cities found that weekends were cloudier than weekdays.
252	There are a few 7 day cycles in nature, but none of them could have this big an effect on weather.'),
253	('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This is a Curious Fact / Explanation type template.

255	Author''s Explanation ... Humans are causing it. (Humans are causing noticeable changes to weather on a large scale)
256	Since the weekend/weekday thing reminds us of a 7 day workweek, and since they''re observing this "weekends are cloudier than weekdays" correlation, the author thinks that humans are causing this.
257	She considers whether there are any natural explanations for this "weekends are cloudier than weekdays" phenomenon but rules them out because the only 7 day cycles that exist are too puny to affect large scale weather.

259	- Is there some OTHER WAY to explain the curious fact?
260	- Is the AUTHOR''S WAY plausible?
261	The author is definitely assuming a difference:
262	"humans do have a 7 day cycle that could cause a big scale weather difference"
263	Necessary Assumption + Curious Fact usually means "ruling out an alternate explanation".
264	How else could we explain the "cloudy weekends" phenomenon? There are no natural explanations that are 7 day cycles.

267	('PT154-S2-Q22', 'single', 0, 'Statements', '```
268	All oil are highly original            O → HO
