275	```'),
276	('PT154-S2-Q22', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'Since we see a lot of quantifiers / conditionals, we can probably expect that our job here is to make a Trait Overlap inference and/or to chain together conditionals.
277	First things first. We always flip negatives to positives:
278	No A''s are B = All A''s are ~B
279	Few A''s are B = Most A''s are ~B
280	Not all A''s are B = Some A''s are ~B
281	That''s why I wrote the two few statements in most language and why I wrote the no statement in all language.
282	Now that are statements are in their more usable form, our #1 priority becomes the conditionals. We got two of them. Do they chain together? Do they have an overlapping idea?
283	O → HO       HO → LC
284	Yes, they both talk about highly original, so we would just get a chain saying
285	Oil → Highly Orig → Late Career
286	~Late Career → ~Highly Orig → ~Oil
287	Now we can look at the other quantified statements and see if any of them are using a trigger from these rules:
288	Most O are ~CA and ~P
289	Nope. This statement ends with ~CA and ~P, neither of which are triggers that allow us to extend that claim.
290	Most A are ~HO
291	Yes! This ends with ~HO, which is a trigger that leads to ~Oil.
292	```
293	Most A are ~HO
294	+ ~HO → ~O
295	---------------------
296	Most A are ~O
297	```'),
298	('PT154-S2-Q22', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'We would expect that the correct answer will either give us the idea that "Most of her abstract works were not oil paintings", or it will be something derivable from the conditional chain we assembled:

300	~Late Career → ~Highly Orig → ~Oil'),

307	('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The artist who painted the self-portrait also painted the battle scene.'),
308	('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'One of the figures in the battle scene closely resembles the young man in the self-portrait.'),
309	('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This argument springs from a Curious Fact / Explanation template:

321	- ? ?'),

333	('PT154-S2-Q25', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'Since Person 2 disagreed with one of Person 1''s explicit claims, we have a pretty good angle on what the correct answer will be (it will sound like Person 1''s first claim, or its negation). 336	Is Babson achieving cultural representation for some culture I don''t recognize? It sounds like it''s from the ahistorical past and future. It''s timeless, but definitely sci-fi.
337	"Babson & Cortez" sounds like a low-rent cop drama on a streaming service you didn''t even realize you had access to, like Crackle. The executives at Sling are praying that their fall lineup is buoyed by Babson & Cortez, "the first cop drama to take place in Connecticut!"
338	Okay, I''m done. But I will make this public service announcement — I think it''s healthy and cathartic to laugh at least once during your LSAT. It releases stress and reminds you that you''re still in charge.'),

343	) AS s(qid, style, position, label, body)
344	JOIN public.lr_questions lq ON lq.qid = s.qid AND lq.preptest = 154
345	JOIN public.lr_explanations ex ON ex.question_id = lq.id AND ex.style = s.style
346	ON CONFLICT (explanation_id, position) DO NOTHING
347	RETURNING explanation_id;

However, the correct answer only needs to strengthen the argument. A principle which states something like, \"a person''s pay should be directly related to how hard they work\" would be good enough. Beware of answers that contain illegal reversals or illegal negations of the assumption we identified above.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'In standing against regimentation and standardization of any sort, Cummings stood against something essential to the work he did (standing for the individual human being).'),\n('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since / and)', 'Metaphor presupposes literal language,\nand\nliteral language essentially involves regimentation.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Excuse me, waiter? Hi, I believe there''s a mistake. I ordered a question 2, but this seems way too hard to read for the 2nd question in an LR section.\nOkay, when we''re confused we can lean on structural clues. We have to find out Conclusion, and \"since\" and \"and\" instruct our eyes that the final two claims are supporting ideas.\nThe conclusion has a pronoun part of it, \"in doing so\", so we need to import language from the first sentence in order to grasp the full claim that the Conclusion is making.\nWhen we''re confused on Sufficient Assumption, we can lean on a trick I call \"Mentioned twice? That''ll suffice.\" The basic idea is that ideas that are mentioned twice (literal language, regimentation, stood against) are pretty much taken care of, in terms of the internal logic of an argument.\nConsider this \"argument\":\nIf A then B. Thus, if A then D, since B presupposes C.\nWithout thinking too hard, we can guess that the missing link is between C and D, since they are only mentioned once, whereas A and B are each mentioned twice.\nSo in this Cummings argument, the stuff only mentioned once is \"essential to the work he did\" and \"metaphor\".\nTo actually understand and predict that link, we should first take advantage of the mini-chain of ideas the Premises are giving us in the final two claims.\nMetaphor presupposes literal language. Literal language involves regimentation.\nMetaphor → literal language → regimentation\nIf you''re against regimentation, as they say Cummings was, then by contrapositive, you would be against literal language and against metaphor.\nThe author is saying, \"If Cummings stood against metaphor, then Cummings stood against something essential to the work he did.\"'),\n('PT154-S2-Q2', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Thus, we want something like \"metaphor was essential to the work Cummings did\".'),\n('PT154-S2-Q6', 'single', 0, 'Given', 'Video games are commonly criticized as morally corrupting in that they allow people to imagine themselves transgressing conventional morality.\nBut it is telling that precisely this criticism was leveled against many forms of popular culture in their early stages of development, including novels, movies, and rock music.\nIt now seems silly to suggest that reading novels is morally corrupting'),\n('PT154-S2-Q6', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'This argument doesn''t contain a clear conclusion indicator word like \"thus,\" or even a clear premise indicator like \"since.\" However, it does seem to follow a common pattern.\nThe first sentence describes a common belief.\nThe next sentence begins with a pivot indicator, \"but.\" The statements that follow the pivot indicator refute the common belief presented in the first sentence.\nWhen we see the phrase \"and just as\" at the beginning of the very last sentence, it indicates that whatever goes in the blank should continue in this direction that the argument is headed: it should continue to refute the idea presented in the first sentence.\nThe argument compares the current criticism of video games to earlier criticism of novels, movies, and rock music, stating that these were also seen as morally corrupting. The argument claims that it now seems silly to criticize novels this way.\nWe would expect this argument to conclude that it seems equally silly to suggest that video games are morally corrupting.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q6', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'A correct answer might indicate that the current criticism of video games is silly. Beware of answers that are too strong as well as ones that seem relevant but actually bring in ideas that go beyond the scope of the stimulus. Also beware of answers that describe unsupported relationships between the concepts in the stimulus.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 0, 'Policy', '```\nNot badly damaged            should not be\nor → removed from\nChecked out w/in            circulation\nlast two years\nWritten by local author\nor considered locally signif            should not be\nand → removed from\nChecked out within            circulation\nlast three years\n```'),\n('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 1, 'Application', 'Paper Flowers should not be removed.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'When we''re given principles, we want to always arrange them so that the Criterion is on the left and the Judgy term (should not be removed) is on the right.\nThe first rule said\nfor X to happen, Y and Z must be true\nWhich contraposes into\nif Y false or Z false, X shouldn''t happen\nThe second rule said\nThings that are Y or Z can be X only if Z\nThat one is too complex to have one standard rule to go off of. We''re better off thinking, \"the default sentiment here is that This group of books can be removed only if they haven''t been checked out for over three years\"\nThat look like\nchecked out within last 3 yrs → can''t be removed\nThen we just added on to the left side what criteria makes you part of \"this group\" of books:\nwritten by local author, significant to local history, or both\nYou could consider fudging the details there and just saying\n```\nLocally important\nand → can''t be removed\nchecked out w/in last 3\n```'),\n('PT154-S2-Q7', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Assuming we symbolized the rules properly, the hard part is over. Since the Application is saying that PF shouldn''t be removed, we can get there by establishing that PF either triggers the first policy or the second.\nSo we either need to hear that PF\n1. isn''t badly damaged\n2. has been checked out in the past 2 years\nor\n3. is \"locally important\" and has been checked out in the past 3 years\nAny of those three conditions will do it.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q8', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (clearly, therefore)', 'Doctors need more training in the treatment of obesity.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q8', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', '45% of docs don''t feel qualified to advise their patients about weight-related issues.\nIf current trends continue, obesity will soon be the leading cause of preventable illness.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q8', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate / Goal', 'We know we want a principle whose outcome sounds like \"then need more training\", because we''re trying to support the idea that docs need more training when it comes to obesity.\nThe trigger just has to be applicable to doctors/obesity. If we were going to predict an \"If / Then\" linking idea, it would sound like,\n\"If 45% of doctors don''t feel qualified to advise their patients about what will soon be the leading cause of preventable illness, then they need more training in the treatment of that cause of illness.\"'),\n('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It''s a myth that a significant amount of music online is the result of people downloading others'' music and reworking it into new music of their own. (i.e. there is not a significant amount of music online that came from people downloading something, sampling it in a new creation, and uploading that creation)'),\n('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', '99% of people who download music do not publish new music of their own on the internet.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'There are a lot of LSAT arguments testing the idea of \"how could a small slice make a big impact?\", and we''re basically supposed to say \"maybe that small slice has a disproportionately large effect on the situation\".\nIf only 1% of people who download music publish new music of their own on the Internet, how could we say a ton of music online came about by people downloading music, sampling it into something they made, and then publishing it?\nMaybe among those 1% are a lot of avid music producers. 99% of us are just downloading, but the 1% are an army of people like Drake and Post Malone, publishing song after song after song.\nTo put it a different way, say there are only 100 people. The author is thinking that since only 1 of them (1%) publishes their music online, then only 1% (an insignificant amount) of the music online could be coming in the form of download/sample/publish.\nBut, if this 1 person creates 50 songs that way, and the other 99 people don''t publish any new music of their own, then when it comes to those 100 people, the only music they''re contributing to the Internet is those 50 songs that were of the download/sample/publish variety.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q9', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer saying that even though 1% is a small percentage of the downloading population, that 1% might be prolific music producers who are responsible for lots of the existing music on the Internet.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q10', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (but - rebuttal)', 'This claim is false.\nrephrase: Even if you don''t get the transmission serviced at least one every three years, most cars won''t develop transmission problems.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q10', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'I''ve owned both of my cars for over 12 years, never had the transmission services, and never developed a transmission problem.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q10', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This argument would be similar to arguing, \"A respected epidemiologist claimed that most people will get a pretty mild case of covid unless they are older than 65 or immunocompromised. But this claim is false. After all, I am younger than 65 with a normal immune system and my case of covid nearly hospitalized me!\"\nWe would respond, \"Sorry to hear that, dude, but you''re just atypical. It''s still true that for most non-geriatric people with normal immune systems, they will have a mild case.\"\nWe can''t fight a generalization with a handful of data points. When we say \"most NBA players are taller than average\", someone can''t argue with us and say, \"Nuh-uh ... average height is 6'' 1\" and several point guards in the NBA are only 6 feet or shorter\".\nSo the flaw in the original argument is thinking that, \"Since MY cars didn''t have transmission problems, even though I never serviced the transmission, the same would be true for MOST cars.\"'),\n('PT154-S2-Q10', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Parallel Flaw loves to test these Famous Flaws (Nec vs. Suff / Part vs. Whole / Sampling / Ad Hominem). Since this argument has a Sampling issue (the author is fighting a genera claim based on a just a couple of nonconfirming data points), let''s look for an argument relying on a sample that is too small.\nThe sample might be from personal experience, as was the case here, but using personal experience isn''t why this is flawed. It''s just because the set of data points yielded by personal experience is so shallow.\nSimilarly, this argument had a rebuttal structure, so the correct answer might, but the Flaw was assuming that \"what was true for this small sample of cases would be true for most cases\", so to replicate the flaw we wouldn''t necessarily need a rebuttal structure.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q11', 'single', 0, 'Information', 'causal (involved in)\nThe amygdala has some influence on how / whether we sense fear.\ncausal (destroys)\nU-W disease destroys the amygdala.\npivot (however)\nPeople with U-W disease generally don''t experience fear, but they experience extreme fear if they''re given a breath that''s high in CO2 (low in oxygen), just as some people with normal amygdala do.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q11', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'Usually when we see pivot wording in Most Supported, it creates some tension between statements. The way we resolve that tension is frequently connected to the correct answer.\nHere, how can we synthesize the idea of people who have U-W disease, who thereby don''t have a working amygdala (which is involved in sensing fear), who still experience fear?\nIs there something special about the fear of suffocating (a breath that is very high in carbon dioxide) that doesn''t involve the amygdala?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q11', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'It''s hard to know what to predict. We just want the most provable answer we can find. But there''s a good chance it''ll have something to do with reconciling the fact that these people without amygdalae are still capable of experiencing fear in certain circumstances.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It is a mistake to criticize the city for being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets.\ntranslation:\nThe city''s not being overzealous (or if they are, we shouldn''t criticize them for it).'),\n('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Can you imagine how much worse parking would be if parking regulations were not enforced?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This is a Straw Man argument, which is a term people use in real life to describe it, but LSAT has never used that wording to describe it. The idea of a Straw Man rebuttal is that you''re unfairly representing the position being debated.\nIf I say, \"I think we should extend unemployment benefits another six months, while we recover from the pandemic\" and you say, \"Well I don''t think it''s the government''s job to make people rich for doing nothing!\" ...\n... you''re not arguing with my position; you''re fighting a Straw Man position you designed, because it makes for an easy opponent (a piñata you can knock down).\nExtending unemployment benefits (which means paying a jobless person around $1200 / month, which is well below the poverty line) is not making people rich for doing nothing. That''s not an intellectually respectful rebuttal to the original position.\nIn this parking ticket argument, the original position in the critics'':\nthe city is being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets.\nThink about what that means. Give yourself a mental picture, a couple examples. What does it mean for a meter maid to be issuing tickets overzealously?\nMaybe the meter runs out for some car at 5:58pm, but the rules for that spot are that you only need to pay until 6pm, after which it''s free. Rather than letting it slide, the overzealous meter maid gives you a ticket for those 2 unpaid minutes.\nMaybe your car is parked a little too far up, in terms of the parking spot lines or in terms of the red part of the curb where you can''t park. Rather than tolerate your front bumper being six inches over the red line, they overzealously give you a ticket for illegal parking.\nThe author''s method of arguing is saying, \"You shouldn''t criticize the status quo, because the alternative is worse!\" You shouldn''t criticize this obnoxiously overzealous ticketing by the meter maids, because the alternative, a wold in which no parking regulations are enforced, would be worse.\nThat''s like a parent handing their kid a bowl of lentil soup, and the kid saying, \"Ouch, that''s too hot\", and the parent responding, \"You shouldn''t criticize. Can you imagine how much worse it would be if it were super cold, with ice cubes in it?\"\nThe kid would properly respond, \"Well, that seems like a False Choice. Why does it have to be scalding hot or unappetizingly cold? Isn''t there a middle ground?\"'),\n('PT154-S2-Q12', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Let''s look for an answer making a similar complaint:\nHey, author, isn''t there a middle ground between overzealous ticketing and no ticketing at all? You can''t say the former is justified because the latter is worse, unless those are our only two options.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It is better that graphic designers not remain anonymous.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Anonymity undermines effective graphic design by making it difficult to hold designers accountable for their work.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'When we''re doing Main Conclusion and Role, we prime our brain with one big tendency before we start reading the paragraph: the vast majority of the time, the Conclusion will come before the Evidence.\nOn Main Conclusion, this manifests in two big patterns:\n- the very first claim of the paragraph is the Main Conclusion\nor\n- the author does a little warm-up with Background or Counterpoint ideas, and then pivots into her Conclusion, usually with a \"but, yet, however\".\nThis argument followed that second pattern, introducing a Background / Counterpoint idea (graphic designers aren''t allowed to sign their works).\nThen it pivots with a \"nonetheless\" into the Conclusion. Then it supports that Conclusion with Evidence.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q13', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'On Main Conclusion, if we found the conclusion, we already did the hard part. Picking a correct answer means that we''re just making ourselves pick whichever answer choice is closer in meaning to the claim we identified (and possibly highlighted) as the Main Conclusion.\nSo we want something that says, \"It is better that graphic designers not remain anonymous\".\nConsider what an equivalent rephrasing might sound like, to make your listening ears more nimble: \"it would be cool if graphic designers did sign their names\" / \"having graphic designers remain anonymous is overall not great\".'),\n('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (Clearly, then)', 'Participation in a work internship decreases the chance that a student will drop out.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Of the high school students who participate in work internships, only 1% drop out (compared to 11% of students overall).'),\n('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author is looking at a curious fact (an asymmetric comparison) and then making a causal conclusion (X decreases the chance of Y).\nCurious Fact ... how come the dropout rate for students in internships is way lower than it is for students overall?\nAuthor''s Causal Explanation ... it must be that participating in the internship causes students to be less likely to drop out.\nOn Flaw (and Weaken and Evaluate and Necessary Assumption), we primarily react to these Explain Curious Fact arguments by thinking, \"could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact?\"\nWith correlations, we''re programmed like robots to think about potential alternate explanations like Reverse Cause / 3rd Factor:\n- Reverse Cause: is it possible that not-dropping out is causing students to have the internship?\n- 3rd Factor: could there be some underlying factor (like good grades) that is causing students to stay in school and causing them to be a part of an internship?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q14', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'With Flaw, we''re mainly trying to call out the fact that the author is overconfidently selling us on one possible causal explanation for a curious fact, without ruling out other possible explanations.\nSo look for an answer to object to the move from a correlation between internships and dropout rates to an assumption that the two are causally related.\nOr look for an answer that discusses an alternate explanation for this association between internships and lower dropout rates.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (Thus)', 'It''s likely that the party now in power will retain power after the upcoming elections.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'Voter confidence in the economy tends to favor incumbent political leaders.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 2, 'Intermediate Conclusion (Clearly)', 'Investors are confident the economy will see increased growth.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 3, 'Evidence', 'Many investors are putting money into stocks.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 4, 'Evaluate', 'There''s a real keyword gumbo going on here: clearly, thus, since. These indicate multiple conclusions, so we theoretically have different reasoning moves to analyze, if we''re looking for assumptions.\nThe logic of all the indicator words works like this:\n```\nDespite\n[counterpoint], opposing idea\nclaim 1. prem supporting 2\nClearly, claim 2. conc, supported by 1.\nThus, conc, supported by 2.\nsince claim 3, prem, supporting 4.\nclaim 4. conc, supp by 2+4\n```\nI typed that and it looks like crazytalk even to me, but each of those indicator words tells you a story of who''s supporting whom or opposed to whom. It''s not a replacement for comprehension, but it''s a way to understand things when you''re not comprehending very well. (sort of like relying on conditional logic indicator words to deal with conditional logic).\nOur author''s two linking assumptions, therefore, would sound like this\n```\nIf ... Then ...\ninvestors are putting       investors are confident\nmoney into stocks → in economic growth\nIf ... Then ...\ninvestors are confident\nin economic growth       The incumbents\nand → will retain power\nvoter confidence tends\nto favor incumbents\n```\nThe first linking assumption was joining claim 1 to claim 2.\nThe second was joining claims 2 and 3 to claim 4.\nMore conversationally, this author is expecting the incumbents to win the upcoming election, because when voters are confident in the economy, incumbents usually win.\nOkay .... are voters confident in the economy?\nThe author would say \"Sure, why just look at how confident investors are right now!\"\nOkay ... does investor confidence match voter confidence?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q15', 'single', 5, 'Goal', 'The correct answer could test the move from \"putting money into stock market\" to \"confident in growth of economy\", but it''s more likely to address the switch the argument makes from talking about investor confidence to talking about voter confidence, while acting like we''re still talking about the same thing.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The rubbish tells us very little about the possession of the people who lived nearby.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The pits have been subject to erosion over long periods of time, with destructive effects on the rubbish within.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This question stem is asking us to strengthen the grounds on which the conclusion is based, so it''s specifically asking us to improve the force / relevance of the evidence.\nIn order for us to figure out what the answer might sound like, we often benefit from thinking about how we''d object to the argument. If we adopt a Weaken mindset, we can get our brain to think of objections, and then those objections become ways to Strengthen the argument (if we rule out or go the opposite direction of those ideas).\ngiven that erosion has had destructive effects on the rubbish within these pits,\nhow could someone still argue that the rubbish does tell us a lot about the possessions of the people who lived nearby?\nI guess we''d just say, \"sure it''s been damaged, but you can still figure out what most of it is ... or you can learn a lot about their potential possessions based on the types of chemical materials you see in there\".'),\n('PT154-S2-Q16', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since want to strengthen the evidence, we would want to rule out these objections we just made.\nCorrect answers could sound like:\n- oh, no you can''t. You can''t make out what any of this stuff is. Too damaged.\n- nope, can''t learn anything important by doing a chemical analysis of the eroded trash.\n- this was the worst kind of erosion'),\n('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (shouldn''t = opinion)', 'The mayor shouldn''t adopt her rival''s controversial proposal for solving the city''s budget problem.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'If she adopts the proposal and it succeeds, she''ll potentially increase her rival''s credibility.\nIf she adopts the proposal and it fails, she''ll get blamed for endorsing a crazy idea.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Structurally, it feels like the the argument is saying:\nCONC: we shouldn''t do X.\nEVID: If X succeeds, something bad happens to us. If X fails, something bad happens to us.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q17', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for a conclusion saying \"we shouldn''t do something\", and premises (probably both conditional) that feel like \"damned if we do, damned if we don''t\".'),\n('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'If it''s correct to define culture as the totality of customs practiced by those whose culture it is, then the customs of a culture necessarily function to preserve it, even if just in a trivial sense.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (because)', 'Under this definition of culture, if a culture were to abandon any custom, then by definition it would destroy that culture.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This author is making a weird, nerdy argument that will make nerdy logicians snap their fingers in nerdy approval.\nAnthropologists are debating whether the customs in a culture help to preserve that culture. (i.e. does celebrating Thanksgiving help to preserve American culture?)\nThe author is not engaging with the substantive truth of whether customs are a way of reinforcing and solidifying cultural identity. Instead, the author is interested in just making an observation about what is technically true, in a vacuous sense.\nSuppose you define a culture as \"the unique set of customs practiced by that culture\".\nCulture X does customs A, B, and C.\nCulture Y does customs A, B, and D.\nEven though they have customs in common, the totality of ABC is different from the totality of ABD, so according to this definition they are each different cultures.\nThe author''s cute observation is that if Culture X were to drop custom C, then they would only do customs A and B. Thus, they would no longer be Culture X, since that culture was defined as the ABC set of customs.\nA culture with an AB set of customs is different from a culture with an ABC set of customs. Thus, getting rid of custom C made the group that used to be Culture X no longer be Culture X.\nAbandoning that custom destroyed their status as Culture X, so, technically, having custom C preserves Culture X.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q18', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We''re being asked about the 2nd sentence. This provides a definition of \"culture\" as \"unique set of customs\" so that the author can make her cute mathematical argument.\nWe could maybe call it a premise, because the author is definitely using it in tandem with the final claim in order to derive the conclusion. But it feels like we could also call it a background claim that the author just scoops up and says, \"If this happened to be true, then inference X would be true because premise Y.\"\nThe answer will probably sound relational (not as simple as premise / conclusion / background / opposing) and convey the idea that the author is just saying, \"by THIS definition, culture definitely preserves culture in a technical, mathematical sense\".'),\n('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (conclude / therefore)', 'The pressure on the nerve in question was causing this patient''s back/leg pain.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'A computer scan suggested there was pressure on a nerve going from the lower vertebra to the leg.\nThis type of pressure can cause inflammation, which can cause pain.\nIf the nerve was inflamed, cortisone would reduce the inflammation. We injected cortisone and then the patient experienced pain relief.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Since the doctor is trying to Solve the Causal Mystery of this patient''s nerve/back pain, we could use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset.\nThe doctor is trying to explain the curious back/leg pain and explains it via pressure on the nerve in question. So we would worry about Alternate Explanations for the back/leg pain and we would worry about the plausibility of the doctor''s diagnosis.\nThe most important moment to notice is when the doctor introduces a conditional claim:\nif the nerve was inflamed, cortisone would help.\nCortisone helped. Thus, the nerve was inflamed.\nWhat''s wrong with that mini-argument?\nWell, that''s an illegal reversal of course. Just because cortisone helped doesn''t show that the doctor''s diagnosis was correct.\nPerhaps there are other possible causes for which cortisone would also help. We haven''t ruled out any of those possibilities.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q19', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer saying something necessary to the internal logic of the doctor''s diagnosis, or look for an answer ruling out the possibility of an alternate diagnosis.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The public''s welfare can be undermined by its own tastes.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (for example ...)', 'Journalists, because they want to focus on stories that stimulate readers, are increasingly doing gossipy stuff rather than hard news.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Whenever we do Main Conclusion questions, we prime our brain to read the paragraph in that special way you read Main Conclusion and Role paragraphs —\nthe conclusion will probably be earlier than the support\nNormally, in Logical Reasoning, the Conclusion is more likely to come at the end of the paragraph. But that tendency big time flips on Main Conclusion and Role. So you start listening as early as the very first sentence.\nYou have to ask yourself actively as you read each claim, \"Is this the conclusion? Does it sound like the author''s opinion? Does the next sentence start off feeling like it''s trying to support this claim?\"\nOn this question, the first sentence doesn''t quite sound like an opinion, but it does sound like an abstract claim, rather than a specific fact. Does the next sentence sound like it''s supporting this initial claim?\nHonestly, no. But if we force ourselves to look closer, there is an overlapping element. They''re both talking about \"the public''s tastes\" / \"the readers'' interest\".\nThe first sentence was positing a causal connection:\npublic''s tastes --> undermines public welfare\nThis second sentence is talking about the public''s taste, when it comes to news stories. Does it say that our taste in news stories leads to \"undermining public welfare\"?\nYes, aloofly. It goes on to complain that serious news stories have been displaced by TMZ and InfoWars type stories (the author is assuming that this switch in emphasis is something that undermines public welfare, but we don''t need to worry about assumptions on Main Conclusion).\nIncidentally the \"Because\" being used in the final sentence should probably be interpreted as a causal usage of Because (as in, \"Because I fell down, my knee is bleeding\"), not a reasoning usage of Because, as a support indicator.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q20', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Our only job is to find the Conclusion. We did. This paragraph made it particularly hard to do so. It''s the first sentence. It sure makes life easier if we know the trick for Main Conclusion questions that the conclusion is earlier than the support, the vast majority of the time.\nLet''s find an answer that mimics the meaning of the first sentence.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It''s now undeniable that humans have an effect on the weather.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'A study of 30 urban cities found that weekends were cloudier than weekdays.\nThere are a few 7 day cycles in nature, but none of them could have this big an effect on weather.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This is a Curious Fact / Explanation type template.\nCurious Fact ... How come in this study, the weekends were cloudier than the weekdays?\nAuthor''s Explanation ... Humans are causing it. (Humans are causing noticeable changes to weather on a large scale)\nSince the weekend/weekday thing reminds us of a 7 day workweek, and since they''re observing this \"weekends are cloudier than weekdays\" correlation, the author thinks that humans are causing this.\nShe considers whether there are any natural explanations for this \"weekends are cloudier than weekdays\" phenomenon but rules them out because the only 7 day cycles that exist are too puny to affect large scale weather.\nWe are thinking\n- Is there some OTHER WAY to explain the curious fact?\n- Is the AUTHOR''S WAY plausible?\nThe author is definitely assuming a difference:\n\"humans do have a 7 day cycle that could cause a big scale weather difference\"\nNecessary Assumption + Curious Fact usually means \"ruling out an alternate explanation\".\nHow else could we explain the \"cloudy weekends\" phenomenon? There are no natural explanations that are 7 day cycles.\nCould it be a 14 day cycle, or a 28 day cycle like the lunar cycle?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q21', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer that needs to be true for it to be plausible that human activity is causing these cloudy weekends, or an answer that rules out an alternate explanation for cloudy weekends.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q22', 'single', 0, 'Statements', '```\nAll oil are highly original            O → HO\nMost oil are not critically            Most O are ~CA and ~P\nacclaimed or popular\nAll highly are late in            HO → LC\noriginal career\nMost abstract are not            Most A are ~HO\nhighly original\n```'),\n('PT154-S2-Q22', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'Since we see a lot of quantifiers / conditionals, we can probably expect that our job here is to make a Trait Overlap inference and/or to chain together conditionals.\nFirst things first. We always flip negatives to positives:\nNo A''s are B = All A''s are ~B\nFew A''s are B = Most A''s are ~B\nNot all A''s are B = Some A''s are ~B\nThat''s why I wrote the two few statements in most language and why I wrote the no statement in all language.\nNow that are statements are in their more usable form, our #1 priority becomes the conditionals. We got two of them. Do they chain together? Do they have an overlapping idea?\nO → HO       HO → LC\nYes, they both talk about highly original, so we would just get a chain saying\nOil → Highly Orig → Late Career\n~Late Career → ~Highly Orig → ~Oil\nNow we can look at the other quantified statements and see if any of them are using a trigger from these rules:\nMost O are ~CA and ~P\nNope. This statement ends with ~CA and ~P, neither of which are triggers that allow us to extend that claim.\nMost A are ~HO\nYes! This ends with ~HO, which is a trigger that leads to ~Oil.\n```\nMost A are ~HO\n+ ~HO → ~O\n---------------------\nMost A are ~O\n```'),\n('PT154-S2-Q22', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'We would expect that the correct answer will either give us the idea that \"Most of her abstract works were not oil paintings\", or it will be something derivable from the conditional chain we assembled:\nOil → Highly Orig → Late Career\n~Late Career → ~Highly Orig → ~Oil'),\n('PT154-S2-Q23', 'single', 0, 'Background', 'If global warming raises the global temperature by as little as 2 degrees Celsius, the seawater will rise and melt the ice caps considerably.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q23', 'single', 1, 'Surprising Claim', 'But in that same scenario, the volume of the ice caps would increase.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q23', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate / Goal', 'Given That\nRising seawater from a warmer planet would melt the ice caps considerably,\nHow Can We Say That\nThe ice caps would grow in volume?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The artist who painted the self-portrait also painted the battle scene.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'One of the figures in the battle scene closely resembles the young man in the self-portrait.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This argument springs from a Curious Fact / Explanation template:\nCurious Fact: who painted this battle scene?\nAuthor''s Explanation: it''s this self-portrait dude.\nWe want to ask ourselves\n- is there some OTHER EXPLANATION for the curious fact?\n- how plausible is the AUTHOR''S EXPLANATION that this self-portrait dude did it?\nThere aren''t really any other causal possibilities teased here, so it''s hard to think about proposing an alternate person who could have painted it. However, we could certainly come up with other explanations for the similarity between the self-portrait and the guy in the battle scene:\n- the dude is just an average looking white guy with a beard, so a lot people of his era closely resembled him\n- maybe the battle scene portrait came first, and the painter got so entranced by this one figure in the battle scene that he decided to pattern his own haircut and facial hair after him, prior to painting the self-portrait\n- someone who is friends with the self-portrait dude decided to paint him in the battle scene\nHow could we make it the author''s story sound less plausible?\n- we could say that the self-portrait artists was only known for painting portraits (hence painting a battle scene wouldn''t likely be in his oeuvre).\n- ? ?'),\n('PT154-S2-Q24', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer that suggests an alternate explanation for who painted the battle scene, an alternate explanation for the similarity between the self-portrait and the dude in the battle scene, or a fact that makes it less likely that the self-portrait artist painted the battle scene.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q25', 'single', 0, 'Babson''s 1st Claim', 'If the quality of the articles / essays is high enough, charging $1 per article/essay will succeed.\nDoes Cortez argue that this plan won''t succeed?\nYes, kinda! He says, \"The fact that [X is the case] is a bad sign for anyone who hopes to earn money this way.\"\nIf X is a fact, and X is a bad sign for those hoping to earn money online by charging $1 per article/essay, then Cortez is conveying pessimism about the prospects of people hoping to make money by charging $1 per article. So they do seem to disagree about B''s first claim!'),\n('PT154-S2-Q25', 'single', 1, 'Babson''s 2nd Claim', 'A dollar ain''t much money.\nDoes Cortez argue it is a lot of money?\nNo, Cortez doesn''t really indicate whether $1 is a lot or a little. He''s only talking about what people are used to spending $1 on vs. what they aren''t used to spending $1 on.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q25', 'single', 2, 'Babson''s 3rd Claim', 'In many countries people routinely tip good service with more than $1.\nDoes Cortez argue that there aren''t many countries where people routinely tip more than $1?\nNo, Cortez definitely seems to agree with this 3rd claim. He is saying, \"True, they do often tip more than $1, but not for stuff like articles.\" So he agrees with Babson on this point.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q25', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'Since Person 2 disagreed with one of Person 1''s explicit claims, we have a pretty good angle on what the correct answer will be (it will sound like Person 1''s first claim, or its negation). We could go deeper and think about what each party is assuming, but let''s cross that bridge if the answer choices for us to.\nIn the meantime, can we talk about the elephant in the room?\nWhat sort of insane name is Babson? Has LSAC written enough questions now that they''ve exhausted all normal names and are determined never to repeat themselves?\nIs Babson achieving cultural representation for some culture I don''t recognize? It sounds like it''s from the ahistorical past and future. It''s timeless, but definitely sci-fi.\n\"Babson & Cortez\" sounds like a low-rent cop drama on a streaming service you didn''t even realize you had access to, like Crackle. The executives at Sling are praying that their fall lineup is buoyed by Babson & Cortez, \"the first cop drama to take place in Connecticut!\"\nOkay, I''m done. But I will make this public service announcement — I think it''s healthy and cathartic to laugh at least once during your LSAT. It releases stress and reminds you that you''re still in charge.'),\n('PT154-S2-Q25', 'single', 4, 'Goal', 'Let''s look for something like Babson''s first claim.\nBabson thinks charging $1 / article online will succeed if the quality is high.\nCortez thinks, regardless of quality, that plan does not have much hope (since people aren''t accustomed to paying for articles).\nBabson, we could say, is assuming that people would be willing to pay for articles.')\n) AS s(qid, style, position, label, body)\nJOIN public.lr_questions lq ON lq.qid = s.qid AND lq.preptest = 154\nJOIN public.lr_explanations ex ON ex.question_id = lq.id AND ex.style = s.style\nON CONFLICT (explanation_id, position) DO NOTHING\nRETURNING explanation_id;"}

We should deeply suspect that this is going to commit the #1 Famous Flaw, Necessary vs. Sufficient, which just means that the author presents a conditional logic premise and then botches using it by making an illegal reversal or negation.
79	The author draws two conclusions, so we should look at each one.
80	The first (intermediate) conclusion is that there must not be abundant gnats. Why? Because there are no geckos, and geckos are found in every environment with abundant gnats.
81	```
82	~Geckos → ~Lots of Gnats
83	Here = ~Geckos
84	Thus, Here = ~Lots of Gnats
85	```
86	This mini-argument seems airtight. It just applies a fact (no geckos) that triggers the contrapositive of the conditional in the first sentence (lots of gnats → geckos).
87	Okay the main conclusion was that the climate is not wet.
88	We were told that gnats survive only in a wet climate. Only / only if are necessary indicators (the only is sufficient), so the idea of "wet climate" goes on the right of the arrow.
89	```
90	Gnats → Wet climate
91	~Wet climate → ~Gnats
92	```
93	The author''s move from the 2nd to last claim to the final claim is, "If there is not an abundant population of gnats, then it must not be a wet climate." But that move looks like this:
94	```
95	~Gnats → ~Wet climate
96	```

121	('PT154-S1-Q12', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'His own current theories presuppose the same assignment he''s criticizing me for.'),
122	('PT154-S1-Q12', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'We could get into the details of Costa said, but they are immaterial to recognizing the Famous Flaw that''s happening here.
123	Whenever we''re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we know to look for these two of the 10 Famous Flaws:
124	1. Ad Hominem — in which an author dismisses the view of person X because person X has some biased interest in the matter or because person X has come conflicting past behavior.
125	2. Unproven vs. Proven False — in which an author concludes that X is false, since there is a lack of evidence that X is true or since someone''s attempt to prove that X is true was a flawed attempt.
126	The conclusion here isn''t saying that Costa is wrong, so we''re not dealing with #2. It''s saying Costa''s view can be discounted / dismissed / disregarded, which is more the style of #1.
127	Why are we discounting Costa''s critique? Because "Costa does the same thing". This is a little like getting busted by Mom for playing with matches and trying to argue that we don''t deserve punishment because "Timmy did the same thing!"
128	Mom will say, "Okay, then both you and Timmy did something wrong."
129	Similarly, we could say to this professor, "Both you and Costa deserve to be criticized for trying to assign works of art to period styles."
130	Someone who commits tax fraud should still be listened to when they accuse person X of committing tax fraud. Someone who had their license suspended for a DUI should still be listened to when they tell person X that X has had too much to drink to get behind the wheel.'),
131	('PT154-S1-Q12', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for something saying, "Just because Costa is guilty of X doesn''t mean we should discount Costa when he argues that we are guilty of X".
132	The answer can also say that the author fails to address the substantive point Costa makes, distracting us instead with irrelevant details about what Costa himself does in his theories.'),
133	('PT154-S1-Q13', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Until voice recognition technology can recognize and utilize grammatical and semantic clues, voice recognition will not accurately translate text.'),
134	('PT154-S1-Q13', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Voice recognition cannot currently accurately tell the difference between homophones.'),
135	('PT154-S1-Q13', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Think about the general form of an argument that says, "Problem X currently prevents us from having Outcome Y. Thus, until we do Action Z, we won''t have Outcome Y."
136	What is being assumed?
137	This is assuming that Action Z is necessary in order to solve Problem X. But what if there are other actions that could also solve Problem X? If that''s the case, then we needn''t say something as harsh as, "If we don''t take Action Z, we''ll never get to Outcome Y."'),
138	('PT154-S1-Q13', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'The author seems to be assuming that, "if we don''t take advantage of grammatical and semantic clues, we''ll never solve the problem of homophones." Or to put it another way, she seems to assume that "solving the problem of homophones requires using grammatical and semantic clues."'),
139	('PT154-S1-Q14', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Later music featuring improv that strays far from the melody shouldn''t be classified as jazz.'),
140	('PT154-S1-Q14', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'In early jazz, the improv didn''t stray too far from the melody.'),
141	('PT154-S1-Q14', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author seems to think that "if early jazz didn''t do it, then doing it disqualifies you from being jazz".
142	If early basketball did not feature a 3-point shot or a 24-second shot clock, then this later basketball, however interesting it may be, featuring 3-point shots and 24-second shot clocks should not be classified as basketball.'),
143	('PT154-S1-Q14', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'This is a silly constraint: only the features of early examples of a genre are allowed within that genre.
144	Look for an answer saying that genres, such as jazz, can evolve. Traits can appear later that didn''t appear before, but still exist within what we currently call that genre.
145	The answer should help us to argue that we CAN classify crazy-improv music as jazz, even when it strays far from the melody.'),
146	('PT154-S1-Q15', 'single', 0, 'Heinrich''s Study', 'It''s aimed at Solving a Causal Mystery, so we can use our Explain Curious Fact mindset if that helps us organize the ingredients.
147	Curious Fact ... Given that ravens survive by eating carcasses and can live for weeks on one carcass, why are they often recruiting more ravens to help them eat one?
148	One Possible Explanation ... The ravens are benevolent altruists thinking, "Why should I eat this carcass by myself for weeks when I could share it with other ravens"?
149	Heinrich''s Explanation ... It''s probably juvenile ravens that that are enlisting other junior ravens in order to chase away a local mated pair that has exclusively claimed the carcass.'),
150	('PT154-S1-Q15', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'What sort of Question Type is this? We might be totally confused about what we''re doing, so the best thing we can do is to re-read the paragraph and get a good understanding of it.
151	Glancing at the answers, we see abstract descriptive language, so we would probably interpret this as something like a Method question, in which we basically need to pick any description that matches.'),
152	('PT154-S1-Q15', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'Pick whichever answer descriptively matches the paragraph.'),
153	('PT154-S1-Q16', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'There is reason to doubt the prevailing view of peasants'' piety.
154	(i.e., not so sure that medieval peasants were deeply dedicated to religion)'),
155	('PT154-S1-Q16', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The record keepers of medieval Europe were clergymen. And it would be in their professional interest to embellish how religiously devoted people were.'),
156	('PT154-S1-Q16', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Given That our source of records of medieval times are clergy members who are biased in favor of exaggerating people''s religious devotion,
157	How Can We Argue That this is no reason to doubt the religious devotion of the medieval peasants (i.e., that the peasants really were religiously devoted)?'),
158	('PT154-S1-Q16', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We either want an answer making us less skeptical of the accuracy of the clergy''s record keeping (it would have been a sin for them to embellish the truth) or an answer that just independently helps us believe that peasants really were religiously devoted.'),
159	('PT154-S1-Q17', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It''s natural that beads, initially just for decoration, came to be used as currency.'),
160	('PT154-S1-Q17', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'A lot of objects that have been used as currency started out solely as decorative objects.'),
161	('PT154-S1-Q17', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'We need to get from "if this has happened before" to "we shouldn''t be surprised it''s happening again".'),
162	('PT154-S1-Q17', 'single', 3, 'Goal', '"If things like X (currency) have previously started out as Y (adornment), then it''s natural for Z (beads), which started as a Y, to be used as X"'),
163	('PT154-S1-Q18', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Constantly repeating simple phrases does not provide extra help to children in learning a language.'),
164	('PT154-S1-Q18', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (we know this because)', 'There are families in which no one speaks to babies this way, yet the children in these families master the grammatical structure of their language just as well as and as quickly as other children do.'),
165	('PT154-S1-Q18', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'There are two big problems with this argument:
166	The conclusion is trying to prove that "X does not help people to do Y", whereas the evidence is only going to establish that "X is not necessary for people to do Y".
167	It''s akin to this flawed argument: "Getting a 175 does not help people get into Harvard. We know this because there are students who got lower then a 175, and they still got into Harvard".
168	We would object to this Harvard argument and to the baby argument the same way — "Just because people can do Y without needing to do X, that doesn''t prove that X isn''t helpful to those who do it."
169	This author is assuming that "If some people don''t need X to do Y, then X isn''t helpful in doing Y."
170	The second problem with the argument is that the conclusion is about whether or not something "helps children to learn a language", whereas the evidence is about whether or not children "mastered grammatical structure as well / as quickly as did other kids".
171	The author seems to think that, "If repeating simple phrases were going to help a kid learn a language, we''d see a difference in terms of how fast/effectively they master grammatical structures." But maybe repeating simple phrases is supposed to help a kid learn a language for a different reason.'),
172	('PT154-S1-Q18', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer speaking to the assumption of, "If some children can learn grammatical structures as well as any other children without having simple phrases repeated to them, then having simple phrases repeated to children doesn''t help them learn a language".
173	Or look for an answer ruling out an objection, where if we negate it, it might allow us to argue that repeating simple phrases does provide extra help to children learning a language.'),
174	('PT154-S1-Q19', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Back belts do not help to prevent back injuries.'),
175	('PT154-S1-Q19', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Everybody at Flegco that does heavy lifting wears back belts, and a study found that employees who wear back belts are more likely to suffer back injuries than those who don''t.'),
176	('PT154-S1-Q19', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'With an Anti-Causal conclusion like this, we''re trying to object by arguing that there is a causal benefit to wearing back belts.
177	Given That
178	the people who wear back belts are more likely to suffer back injuries than those who don''t
179	How Could We Argue That
180	back belts do help prevent back injuries.
181	Our normal objections for Anti-Causal arguments are:
182	- the two groups aren''t fair to compare to begin with
183	- things would be even worse without X (in this case, back belts)
184	In this argument, we already know one salient difference between people who do / don''t wear back belts: the people who do wear back belts have work that involves lifting heavy objects. The people who don''t wear back belts don''t have to lift heavy objects.
185	I''m not surprised to hear that "people, wearing back belts, moving furniture all day" have more back injuries than "people, not wearing back belts, sitting at a computer all day".
186	This would be like arguing, "People who wear bulletproof vests are actually more likely to die of gunshot wounds than are people who don''t wear bulletproof vests. Thus, bulletproof vests don''t help to prevent death from gunshot wounds."
187	Of course they do — it''s just that people who wear bulletproof vests have jobs that put them in the line of fire, so their chances of dying from a gunshot wound are much, much higher to begin with than are people with other jobs.'),
188	('PT154-S1-Q19', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Find an answer saying that the author is failing to consider that things would be way worse without back belts for all these people doing heavy lifting, or pointing out the objection that it''s unfair to compare the likelihood of back injuries of belt-wearers vs. non-belt-wearers, since the former have such a higher chance of getting back injuries in the first place based on the nature of the heavy lifting work they do.'),
189	('PT154-S1-Q20', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Shakespeare probably came to know E''s play Alcestis via a Latin translation.'),
190	('PT154-S1-Q20', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Shakespeare knew little to no Greek, so it''s unlikely he read the original Greek version of Alcestis.'),
191	('PT154-S1-Q20', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This is the Curious Fact / Explanation template that shows up so much on Strengthen and Weaken.
192	Curious Fact: How was Shakespeare exposed to Alcestis?
193	Author''s Story: He read a Latin translation
194	To strengthen a causal argument, we want to
195	1. Help Author''s Explanation
196	or
197	2. Rule Out Alternate Explanation'),
198	('PT154-S1-Q20', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Get rid of four answers that Strengthen (the correct answer may weaken or may just be neutral).
199	They should either help us believe that Shakespeare learned Alcestis via a Latin translation, or rule out a different way that Shakespeare might have learned Alcestis.'),
200	('PT154-S1-Q21', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'It should come as no surprise that Marva''s food is great will Traintrack''s food is ordinary.'),
201	('PT154-S1-Q21', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Traintrack''s has a more convenient location than Marva''s, and it by itself is almost enough to guarantee a steady flow of customers.'),
202	('PT154-S1-Q21', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This is a super tricky conclusion to deal with, "the discrepancy should come as no surprise" is a mouthful. Much of the time, the conclusion on a Principle-Strengthen question would be something like "impermissible" / "should be avoided", and so we predict an answer that looks like
203	such-and-such → impermissible
204	Here we''d be predicting something like
205	such-and-such → discrepancy should come as no surprise
206	We might want to approach that conclusion with a more flexible mindset. What are some other ways to say "X should come as no surprise"?
207	- X is what you''d expect
208	- X frequently occurs
209	- it''s natural for X to occur
210	Because Traintrack''s has a more convenient location than Marva''s
211	We would expect Traintrack''s to have worse food than Marva''s
212	or, equivalently
213	Because Marva''s has a less convenient location than Traintrack''s
214	We would expect Marva''s to have better food than Marva''s'),
215	('PT154-S1-Q21', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We need to pick an answer that establishes a clear connection between how convenient your location is (whether it guarantees customers) and how good your food is.
216	If we get a little gist-y and real world with this, our author is just saying, "Nobody expects great food from the diner at the train station, or a restaurant in an airport or amusement park. They have built-in crowds who are desperate for food, so they can overcharge and underperform."'),
217	('PT154-S1-Q22', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Increases in produce prices have led to an increase in the planting of personal gardens.'),
218	('PT154-S1-Q22', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The two largest retail seed companies each reported sales increases of 19% last year, a year in which the price of produce spiked.'),
219	('PT154-S1-Q22', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The "apparently" in the first sentence is the Opinion Indicator that shows it''s our conclusion, plus the second idea is past tense and quantitative, making it seem factual.
220	This has the Curious Fact / Explanation structure we see so often in Strengthen / Weaken questions.
221	Curious Fact: Why did the two biggest retail seed companies report 19% increases in sales last year?
222	Author''s Story: Last year, the price of produce spiked, so people decided to start growing produce at home in personal gardens, so they bought a bunch of seeds from these two large companies.
223	The pressure points:
224	- how ELSE could we explain the Curious Fact
225	- how plausible is the Author''s Story'),
226	('PT154-S1-Q22', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since this is Weaken, we would either want
227	1. a different reason why the two companies have +19% in sales (maybe they''re selling something other than seeds / maybe some of their competitors went out of business so that now they have a bigger market share)
228	or
229	2. something that undermines the storyline that people reacted to high produce prices at the supermarket by thinking, "I should grow my own at home ... let me go by some seeds from a big box seed retailer".'),
230	('PT154-S1-Q23', 'single', 0, 'Statements', '```
231	conditional = "all" (LEFT side)
232	Highly successful → main desire leave mark
233	entrepreneur
234	conditional = "whenever" (LEFT side)
235	Highly successful       implement
236	entrepreneur sees → that
237	solution to problem       solution
238	conditional = "all" (LEFT side)
239	~Highly successful → don''t always have
240	entrepreneur       motivation to
241	implement ideas
242	```'),
243	('PT154-S1-Q23', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'When we''re given multiple conditionals, we want to see if they chain together. Is there any overlapping idea?
244	Sort of ... highly successful entrepreneurs (or people who aren''t) show up in all three conditionals.
245	It might be easier to think of this in a Comparison way:
246	```
247	Highly Succ E''s        Not Highly Succ E''s
248	main desire ?        ?
249	leave mark on world
250	see solution?        see solution?
251	implement it.        might not be
252	                     motivated enough
253	                     to implement it
254	```'),
255	('PT154-S1-Q23', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'Pick something we can derive from this comparison or from these conditionals.'),
256	('PT154-S1-Q24', 'single', 0, 'Given that ...', 'Nuts are high in calories, and that the more calories you consume, the more likely you become overweight.'),
257	('PT154-S1-Q24', 'single', 1, 'How is it that ...', 'People who regularly eat nuts are less likely to be overweight than people who never eat nuts.'),
258	('PT154-S1-Q24', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate / Goal', 'Since these nut-eaters have a high calorie habit, which skews towards being overweight, we need to know something else about nut-eaters that skews towards not being overweight (maybe they exercise more / maybe they have higher metabolisms / maybe they eat less of other high-calorie foods like desserts).
259	Look for a difference between nut-eaters and nut-avoiders that relates to weight gain / weight loss / burning calories.'),
260	('PT154-S1-Q25', 'single', 0, 'Premise 1 (before/after correlation)', 'Each major earthquake has been preceded by minor tremors.
261	Each X has been preceded by Y.'),
262	('PT154-S1-Q25', 'single', 1, 'Premise 2 (fact)', 'The region has been experiencing minor tremors.
263	Y has been happening.'),
264	('PT154-S1-Q25', 'single', 2, 'Conclusion (certain prediction)', 'An earthquake''s a ''comin.
265	X will happen'),
266	('PT154-S1-Q25', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'It wouldn''t be unreasonable to conclude something like, "So there might be an earthquake coming", but we can''t conclude so certainly that an major quake will come.
267	We know that every quake has been preceded by tremors, but it''s possible that many times there have been tremors that weren''t followed by an earthquake.
268	We could understand this flaw as a Necessary vs. Sufficient error, if we think of that first premise as a conditional.
269	Each is a sufficient indicator (left side idea), so we''d have:
270	Major quake → preceded by tremors
271	The argument is thinking, "if tremors, then major earthquake."'),
272	('PT154-S1-Q25', 'single', 4, 'Goal', 'Look for something that feels like an illegal backwards move, either with conditional logic, or with a before/after.'),
273	('PT154-S1-Q26', 'single', 0, 'Statements (conditionals — only if = necessary / right)', '```
274	Morally → action performed
275	responsible       freely
276	action performed → alternative action that''s
277	freely       genuinely open to person
278	alternative action → performing that action
279	genuinely open       is not morally wrong
280	```'),
281	('PT154-S1-Q26', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'Whenever we see conditionals in Inference questions, we want to ask ourselves two things:
282	1. Are there any other conditionals I could chain this up with?
283	2. Are there any facts I could apply this to (via contrapositive usually)
284	It seems like all three of these claims could chain together since the 1st and 2nd sentences both mention free actions, and the 2nd and 3rd both mention an alternative action that''s genuinely open.
285	```
286	Morally       action       alt. action       alt. action is
287	responsible → freely → genuinely → not morally
288	for action       performed       open       wrong
289	```
290	Let''s put this front to back in conversational times. If you''re morally responsible for action X, then you freely performed X, which means you had a morally acceptable action Y that was an alternative action genuinely open to you.
291	Meanwhile, if there''s no other morally acceptable action available to you when you do action X, then you''re not morally responsible for X.'),
292	('PT154-S1-Q26', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'Look for anything provable, but expect the correct answer to reward us for linking together that chain, and look for trap answers that do illegal reversals or negations.')
293	) AS s(qid, style, position, label, body)
294	JOIN public.lr_questions lq ON lq.qid = s.qid AND lq.preptest = 154
295	JOIN public.lr_explanations ex ON ex.question_id = lq.id AND ex.style = s.style
296	ON CONFLICT (explanation_id, position) DO NOTHING
297	RETURNING explanation_id;

{"project_id": "53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76", "sql": "WITH ins AS (\nINSERT INTO public.lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id, position, label, body)\nSELECT ex.id, s.position, s.label, s.body\nFROM (VALUES\n('PT154-S1-Q1', 'single', 0, 'Rhett''s Claims', '1. Otto gives me a ride to work every morning.\n2. Otto expects me to help pay for fuel.\n3. I shouldn''t have to pay.\n4. Giving me a ride doesn''t increase the amount he spends on fuel.\n5. He has to go right by my house anyway.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q1', 'single', 1, 'Barbara''s Responses', '1. no comment\n2. no comment\n3. Disagrees\n4. no comment (possibly agrees)\n5. no comment'),\n('PT154-S1-Q1', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Barbara doesn''t dispute any of the facts that Rhett presents as evidence, such as Rhett''s claim #1, #2, or #5. She might even agree withe claim #4, because she mentions diverting warm air from her house \"without increasing my expenses.\" So, when Barbara states that Rhett''s logic is \"flawed,\" she seems to be disagreeing with his conclusion that he should not have have to pay Otto.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q1', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We''ll look for an answer that addresses claim #3.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q2', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (Thus)', 'If the world has ever known true democracy, it was only in ancient Athens.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q2', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'It was only in ancient Athens that the legislature consisted of all eligible voters. Athenian voters made all political decisions by direct vote after public debate in the Assembly.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q2', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The conclusion we''re trying to prove is a weird type of claim. Suppose I said, \"Thus, if Shelly has any flowers in her kitchen right now, she has daisies\". I''m not actually trying to prove she has daisies. I''m saying daisies are the only possible type of flower in her kitchen, but it wouldn''t make me wrong if there weren''t daisies. I would only be wrong if there were some other type of flower in her kitchen.\nSo, similarly, we don''t need to prove that ancient Athens was a true democracy. We just need to know that every other political system has not been a true democracy.\nSince the argument didn''t give us a proving mechanism for \"If xyz, then not true democracy\", the correct answer will have to give us such a rule.\nWhat do we know about all other political systems that would allow us to prove they aren''t true democracies? We know that only ancient Athens had a legislature consisting of all eligible voters. So in every other political system, we know that wasn''t the case.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q2', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'So, we''re probably going to see an answer like, \"If a political system didn''t have a legislature consisting of all eligible voters, then it wasn''t a true democracy\".'),\n('PT154-S1-Q3', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (not a waste = opinion)', 'Government funding of NEO research is not a waste of money.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q3', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Buying home insurance makes good fiscal sense, and governments fund NEO research for the same reason that people insure their homes (it''s designed to develop measures to counteract a possible hit by a sizable NEO).'),\n('PT154-S1-Q3', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Whenever we''re trying to find the conclusion on Role and Main Conclusion, we remind ourselves that 90% of the arguments on those two question types are \"upside-down\" arguments: we hear the Conclusion before we hear the Evidence.\nSo we have to listen keenly for Opinion indicators or Rebuttals. The first two sentences of this argument provide some background context so that we know what NEO research is all about. Then the 3rd sentence gives us the author''s opinion: government funding of NEO research is \"worth it / not a waste\".'),\n('PT154-S1-Q3', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We''re being asked about the 2nd to last claim, which was one of the two premises. (99% of the time that two claims are joined by and, they are both premises). It also happens to be invoking an analogy between buying home insurance and funding NEO research.\nSo let''s look for something like \"an analogy / premise that supports the 3rd sentence\".'),\n('PT154-S1-Q4', 'single', 0, 'Given Oscar''s Claims', '1. Student evaluations provide the best assessment of teacher performance.\n2. Who is in a better position to judge a teacher than that teacher''s students? [This is a rhetorical question. It indicates that Oscar believes students are in the best position to judge a teacher.]'),\n('PT154-S1-Q4', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate Bettina''s Responses', '1. Disagrees\n2. no comment\nIt''s interesting to note that Bettina doesn''t necessarily disagree that students are in the best position to judge a teacher. What Bettina is saying is that students'' impressions at the end of a class might not accurately reflect the impact that the teacher has. Students judgements might be valid and accurate, but possibly not until many years later.\nBettina''s statement about peer evaluations being a better alternative directly refutes Oscar''s claim about student evaluations being best.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q4', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'We''ll look for an answer that addresses claim #1. There were more details in Bettina''s statements than in Oscar''s, so beware of answers which focus on details about which Oscar never expressed an opinion.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q5', 'single', 0, 'Evidence', 'Taste buds were the primary tool early humans used for testing foods.\nSour taste warns of possible spoilage of food while bitterness is a warning of many poisons.\nEarly humans also recognized sweet foods and salty foods as meeting nutritional needs.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q5', 'single', 1, 'Conclusion', 'The fact that people can now clearly distinguish these four tastes— sour, bitter, sweet, and salty— is completely explained by people''s use of taste to test for the healthfulness of foods.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q5', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Wow! Completely explained? That''s a mighty strong conclusion.\nThe evidence describes details about food that would have been important for early humans to know, like possible spoilage, and describes a correlation between these details and certain tastes like sourness and bitterness.\nThis correlation doesn''t guarantee that needing to know about spoilage caused our ability to detect a sour taste. It doesn''t guarantee that the need for nutrients caused our ability to detect sweetness or saltiness.\nThe evidence doesn''t guarantee that this one use for our sense of taste completely explains our ability to distinguish these four tastes. The ability to distinguish these four tastes could have developed for more than one reason.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q5', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since this is such a strong conclusion, a correct answer only needs to point out that there could be more than one reason for our ability to distinguish these four tastes.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q6', 'single', 0, 'Given that …', 'The city raised on-street parking rates in a downtown business district in order to discourage congestion.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q6', 'single', 1, 'Why is it that …', 'Restaurants in the district that require short-term parking ended up having a small increase in sales.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q6', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Raising the price of parking meters is supposed to discourage people from street parking; the fewer cars you have driving around searching for an open meter, the less congestion you''ll theoretically have.\nBut after this city raised the price of parking meters, restaurants whose customers have to find short-term parking did even a little better than before. We would expect these restaurants to struggle, now that customers who want to go there are thinking, \"Wait a sec … isn''t that the place that requires street parking? Didn''t they just raise the rates of the meters? Let''s go somewhere else\"'),\n('PT154-S1-Q6', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We could try to predict an answer, but on Paradox that''s borderline risky, since the correct answer might surprise us.\nLet''s just look for a way to explain something that changed since the meters got more expensive that could somehow slightly benefit restaurants in the area.'),\n('PT154-S1-Q7', 'single', 0, 'Given Mark''s Claims', '1. Advances in technology have caused the decline of newspaper sales.\n2. News can be reported in articles on the Internet as it happens and at any time of day.\n3. What should we be thinking? Why? Because there are no geckos, and geckos are found in every environment with abundant gnats.\n```\n~Geckos → ~Lots of Gnats\nHere = ~Geckos\nThus, Here = ~Lots of Gnats\n```\nThis mini-argument seems airtight. It just applies a fact (no geckos) that triggers the contrapositive of the conditional in the first sentence (lots of gnats → geckos).\nOkay the main conclusion was that the climate is not wet.\nWe were told that gnats survive only in a wet climate. Only / only if are necessary indicators (the only is sufficient), so the idea of \"wet climate\" goes on the right of the arrow.\n```\nGnats → Wet climate\n~Wet climate → ~Gnats\n```\nThe author''s move from the 2nd to last claim to the final claim is, \"If there is not an abundant population of gnats, then it must not be a wet climate.\" But that move looks like this:\n```\n~Gnats → ~Wet climate\n```\nThat''s not what the rule said. Unproven vs. Proven False — in which an author concludes that X is false, since there is a lack of evidence that X is true or since someone''s attempt to prove that X is true was a flawed attempt.\nThe conclusion here isn''t saying that Costa is wrong, so we''re not dealing with #2. It''s saying Costa''s view can be discounted / dismissed / disregarded, which is more the style of #1.\nWhy are we discounting Costa''s critique? Because \"Costa does the same thing\". people can do Y without needing to do X, that doesn''t prove that X isn''t helpful to those who do it.\"\nThis author is assuming that \"If some people don''t need X to do Y, then X isn''t helpful in 33	('PT154-S4-Q4', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (therefore)', 'The law is inadequate as a means of giving the English people access to their archaeological heritage.'),
34	('PT154-S4-Q4', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The law requires finders of valuable antiquities to offer them to English museums at reasonable prices.
