51	('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (So / probably)', 'If people in industrialized societies undertook corresponding group tasks, their communities would probably become more close-knit.'),
52	('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 1, 'Intermediate Conclusion', 'Much of what made medieval European communities as close-knit as they usually were was the way they went about meeting basic needs.'),
53	('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 2, 'Evidence', 'In medieval communities, to get crops harvested or to dig a well, people had to come together in respectful cooperation, despite any private grievances they might have had.'),
54	('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'On Main Conclusion and Role, the main conclusion is earlier than the evidence about 85-90% of the time (it''s usually the very first sentence or attached to some pivot/rebuttal).
55	But here we looking at the 10% of the time where the Conclusion is actually the last claim in the paragraph, on a Main Conclusion question. (This tends to only happen on easier Main Conclusion questions early in the LR section, such as Q6 like this one)
56	We know the word "So" indicates conclusions. The word "probably" often indicates an Opinion. Both of those words show up in the final sentence.
57	When we think we''ve found the Conclusion, we should test whether it is actually supported, by asking why should we believe that?
58	Why should we believe that we modern industrialized societies would probably become more close-knit if we undertook group tasks with the community?
59	Because ... much of what made Medieval societies close-knit was the group tasks they undertook together (such as harvesting crops or digging a well). It forced them to come together in respectful cooperation, suspending any private grievances.'),
60	('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 4, 'Goal', 'Okay, we confirmed that the last sentence is the conclusion. It is wearing a conclusion indicator (So); it is expressing an opinion the author has (probably); and it is supported by the other two claims in the paragraph.
61	So let''s go find an answer that matches the meaning of the final sentence.'),
62	('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 0, 'Evidence', 'Applying a copper solution to blighted plants is an effective treatment for the disease [late blight].'),
63	('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 1, 'Conclusion (Therefore)', 'Late blight must be caused by a deficiency of copper in the plant''s mineral intake.'),
64	('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'LR questions often contain arguments that contain evidence about a correlation—two things that tend to happen together. Based on just that correlation, the argument claims that one of the things causes another. This is called a Causal Flaw, which is often explained by saying "a correlation does not prove causation."
65	This argument''s conclusion claims that late blight is caused by a copper deficiency. But the evidence is a bit stronger than a mere correlation. The copper solution is described as an "effective treatment." This indicates that the copper solution causes the plants to be cured of the disease.
66	So, what''s the exact flaw here? The conclusion implies that tomato plants need copper. The assumption is that the copper solution provides the plants with this copper that they need. But none of that is stated in the evidence. We''re never told what the copper solution actually does, only that it works. It certainly might cure a copper deficiency, but there are other, equally plausible alternative explanations.
67	For example, late blight could be caused by some type of organism that is killed by the copper solution. If this is true, the conclusion doesn''t really make sense. It would be the same as saying, "Antibiotic cream is an effective treatment for an infected wound. Therefore, infected wounds are caused by a deficiency of antibiotic cream in a person''s diet."'),
68	('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer with evidence that describes some type of causal connection without explaining exactly how that connection works. The conclusion will be based on an assumption that there is only one explanation, when other reasonable possibilities exist. Watch out for answers containing arguments that aren''t flawed, or ones that contain a completely different flaw.'),

85	('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Mountain climbing isn''t as dangerous as the popular media likes to claim.'),
86	('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'From 1922 - 2002, there were less than 200 climbing fatalities on Mount Everest, which is one of the most dangerous mountains in the world. In 2002, alone, there were 7,000 traffic fatalities in France.'),
87	('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Given this crazy asymmetry between deaths on Everest vs. those on the roadways of France, how could we still argue that mountain climbing is as dangerous as the popular media has said (which we''ll assume is "quite dangerous"?)
88	One move we have is saying, "Cool, well the roadways in France are crazy dangerous, so no wonder there are so many deaths. Just because mountain climbing isn''t as dangerous, that doesn''t mean it''s not dangerous!"
89	If you claimed that the Empire State Building in NYC was a really tall building, and someone said "No, it''s not. There''s a couple buildings in Dubai that are taller", that''s not a good objection.
90	The popular media didn''t say mountain climbing is the most dangerous thing ever, so pointing out that something else is potentially more dangerous is a Relative vs. Absolute type flaw.
91	But we should also think about how unfair the comparison is. If I tried to argue that "skydiving is more dangerous than flying in an airplane", and you said, "Nuh-uh — more people die in airplanes each year", it''s not a fair comparison.
92	We don''t measure danger in terms of the raw number of injuries / fatalities. We judge danger in terms of the likelihood of getting an injury / fatality.
93	If 13% of skydivers die each year, but only 1% of airplane riders die, then it seems like skydiving is more dangerous, regardless of the absolute number of deaths. 157	('PT154-S4-Q18', 'single', 0, 'Statements', '```

169	('PT154-S4-Q18', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'When we''re given conditionals in Inference questions, we''re asking ourselves whether they chain together with other conditionals or whether there are any facts we''re provided with that would trigger them (or, more often, trigger their contrapositives).
170	Here, we''re given a rule that says, "If protection were capable of making our companies big enough to compete with foreign rivals, then it would have already done so within 10 years of protection".
171	Then we get the facts that the protection has already been in place for 10 years and the companies are still asking for more protection so that they can grow big enough.
172	So by the contrapositive of the author''s conditional, we can derive that apparently "protection from foreign competition is a NOT a possible way to enable the machinery firms to grow big enough to successfully compete".'),

The people who are scared of COVID aren''t leaving their house, so you''re not getting a chance to interview them."

181	('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'No serious novelist is motivated primarily by the desire to make money.'),
182	('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'All serious novelists care about literary style.
183	The more a novel appeals to the general public, the more money its author will make.'),
184	('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'CONVERSATIONAL TAKE
185	The conclusion is making a connection between the concepts of "serious novelist" and "desire to make money".
186	Serious novelist is a concept the evidence defines / discusses. We know that all of them care about literary style.
187	Okay, so the author''s conclusion is thinking that "someone who cares about literary style is not motivated by the desire to make money".
188	The evidence never defines "desire to make money", but it does talk about money. We know that more appealing novels make more money. So if you desired money, we know you''d try to make more appealing novels.
189	If you didn''t desire money, you wouldn''t care if you novels were appealing.
190	"Someone who cares about literary style is not motivated by the desire to (does not care if they don''t) make money."
191	So, someone who cares about literary style doesn''t care if their novels are appealing? Yes, the author must be thinking that serious novelists care about literary style, which they know the general public doesn''t find appealing, so their books aren''t as likely to make money. So the author infers that serious novelists must not be motivated by a desire to make money.
192	FORMAL TAKE
193	The conclusion defines this logic path as our objective:
194	```
195	Serious --------------------------> not primarily
196	Novelist       motiv by $
197	```
198	The premise gives us this part of the path:
199	```
200	Serious -----> cares about
201	Novelist       literary style
202	```
203	So the missing ingredient the answer needs to provide to complete our logic path:
204	```
205	cares about ----> not primarily
206	literary style       motiv by $
207	```'),
208	('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We need to prove "primary motivation is not money" about serious novelists, and the only we know for sure about serious novelists is that they care about literary style. Look for an answer that allows us to get from "cares about literary style" to "not primarily motivated by money".'),
209	('PT154-S4-Q21', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The new computer control systems will probably only make the problems with accidental air bag inflation worse.'),
210	('PT154-S4-Q21', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The new system will be more complex, and the more complex a system is, the more ways there are in which it can fail.'),
211	('PT154-S4-Q21', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author is essentially making a move from "there are more ways the system can fail" to "it is more likely that the system will fail".
212	Mathematically, that doesn''t have to be true.
213	Say there are only two ways to fail, and each way of failing has about a 30% chance of occurring.
214	Avoiding failure would mean that both things go right, which is 70% probability times 70% probability
215	7/10 * 7/10 = 49/100 = 49% chance it doesn''t fail.
216	No say there are four ways to fail, but each of them only has about a 10% chance of occurring.
217	Avoiding failure would mean that all four things go right, is 90% * 90% * 90% * 90%.
218	[checks calculator] = 65% chance it doesn''t fail.'),
219	('PT154-S4-Q21', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Find an answer saying that "just because there are more ways to fail doesn''t mean that a system is more likely to fail", or one saying "fails to consider that the probability of any of these myriad ways the new system could fail is much lower than the probability was that the old system could fail".'),
220	('PT154-S4-Q22', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Environmental factors clearly have little effect on whether a teenager will participate in sports.'),
221	('PT154-S4-Q22', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The strongest environmental factor is probably family life, yet it''s common for one teenager in a family to participate in sports while others in the same family don''t like sports.
222	Also, school programs designed to get inactive teens to play sports are generally ineffective.'),
223	('PT154-S4-Q22', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'We have an Anti-Causal conclusion, so we''ll be trying to argue that "environmental factors DO have much effect on whether teens participate in sports".
224	We can weaken the argument by "making excuses" for the author''s premises or by introducing new evidence that helps us say "environmental factors do have a lot of effect".
225	Given That
226	Within a family, some teens play sports and others don''t
227	How Could We Argue That
228	family life is operating as an environmental factor?
229	We would say a number of things:
230	- who says that the family is trying to influence all its kids in the same direction? (maybe you have an old-school Dad who tries to move his son towards sports but moves his daughters away from sports).
231	- what if the younger siblings want to differentiate themselves from the older sibling that got into sports?
232	Given That
233	School programs that try to get inactive teens into playing sports usually don''t work,
234	How Could We Argue That
235	environmental factors do influence whether teens play sports?
236	Maybe we could say that these cheesy assemblies exert influence, just in an opposite, backfiring direction. Maybe teens are being convinced out of getting into high school sports based on who unappealing these school programs are.
237	Or we could just say that these programs are poorly designed and that if properly designed they would be able to exert some environmental influence.'),
238	('PT154-S4-Q22', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer "explaining away" either of the two premises in a way that protects the idea that environmental factors are playing a role, or for any answer that helps us argue that environmental factors are playing a role.'),
239	('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Talking on a cell phone while driving is more dangerous than talking with someone in the vehicle while driving.'),
240	('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 1, 'Intermediate Conclusion', 'The person on the phone can''t immediately recognize when driving is becoming difficult.'),
241	('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 2, 'Evidence', 'The person on the phone can''t see the driving situation. Meanwhile, a conversation partner inside the vehicle will usually shut up or say something helpful if it''s a difficult driving situation.'),
242	('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'There is a missing link in going from the first half of the second sentence to the second half of the second sentence:
243	if you can''t see a driving situation, then you can''t immediately recognize when driving becomes difficult.
244	This is pretty reasonable, but one could attack its veracity — maybe you can hear a difference in the person you''re talking to and that tells you that they are confronting a difficult driving situation.
245	Nevertheless, it''s probably not that likely that LSAC is testing that assumption.
246	The bigger gap would be getting from "what''s different" between cell phone conversation and in-car conversation to "more / less dangerous".'),
247	('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 4, 'Goal', 'The author is assuming that "if conversation partner keeps talking during difficult driving situation, then it''s more dangerous", or, equivalently, "if someone shuts up or offers helpful warnings during a difficult driving situation, then it''s less dangerous."'),
248	('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Experiencing a traumatic event can affect how much cortisol one produces in response to stress.'),
249	('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'People who have experienced trauma (w/o PTSD) tend to produce more cortisol when exposed to stress than do people who haven''t experienced trauma.'),
250	('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Since the evidence is a Curious Comparison and the conclusion is inferring some sort of Causal claim, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset.
251	Curious Fact ... Why is it that people who have had a traumatic event produce higher levels of cortisol when exposed to stress?
252	Author''s Explanation ... Experiencing a traumatic event apparently changed how much cortisol their bodies produce in response to stress.
253	The two pressure points are always
254	- Alternate Explanations for the curious fact
255	- the plausibility of the Author''s Explanation
256	How about that whole PTSD thing. Did that matter?
257	Hard to say. It certainly feels like a detail-clue they wouldn''t accidentally put in there if they weren''t planning to use it.
258	People who experienced a traumatic event but do subsequently develop PTSD are not involved in the curious comparison.
259	There are three groups, but we''re only comparing the first two:
260	no trauma | trauma, no PTSD | trauma, yes PTSD'),
261	('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since this is Weaken, the correct answer is more likely to be an Alternate Explanation for why people who have experienced trauma tend to produce more cortisol in response to stress.
262	But it might undermine the plausibility that experiencing the trauma changed their cortisol response.'),
263	('PT154-S4-Q25', 'single', 0, 'Statements', 'From 1960 to 2010, the fishing industry was able to harvest a greater percentage of the total amount of fish, by weight, in the world''s oceans, in each succeeding year.
264	The harvest, by weight, increased steadily from 1960 to 1995, but didn''t increase after 1995.'),
265	('PT154-S4-Q25', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'We usually read Must Be True looking for Conditional, Causal, or Math-y type ideas. This one seems to giving us Math-y ideas, so reconciling the first and second sentences will give us some mathematical inference.
266	We know that the fishing industry caught a higher percentage of the weight of the fish in the ocean each year.
267	Let''s pretend there are 1,000 tons of fish in the ocean each year.
268	```
269	       % caught   tons caught
270	1960   10%        100
271	1965   11%        110
272	1970   12%        120
273	....
274	1995   20%        200
275	2000   25%        200
276	2005   50%        200
277	```
278	We complied with two different constraints there:
279	1. percentage keeps increasing from 1960 to 2010
280	2. overall weight of fish caught increases from 1960 to 1995
281	3. overall weight of fish caught stops increasing from 1995 to 2010
282	But notice something funky with our numbers. For a while they kept equaling 1000 tons of fish in the sea, but once the percentage goes up and the tonnage caught stays the same, it means that the total weight of fish in the sea has to start decreasing.
283	```
284	       % caught   tons caught   total in sea
285	1960   10%        100           1000
286	1965   11%        110           1000
287	1970   12%        120           1000
288	....
289	1995   20%        200           1000
290	2000   25%        200           800
291	2005   50%        200           400
292	```
293	The inference they''re going for here is the same as this:
294	my rent has stayed the same for the past 10 years, but nowadays my rent is a bigger percentage of my income.
295	What can we infer?
296	My income is lower now than it used to be.
297	If I''m paying a higher tax rate this year than last year, but the government is still getting the same money from me, then that means my taxable income has gone down.'),
298	('PT154-S4-Q25', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'We don''t know for sure what''s going on from 1960 through 1995. It''s possible that the overall weight of fish in the ocean goes down, stays the same, or goes up.
299	But once we get to 1995, we know for sure that the total weight of fish in the ocean starts going down, because the % of available fish weight being caught keeps rising but the actual weight of fish being caught does not increase.'),
300	('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'If there''s no methane on Planet 253, then there''s no life on Planet 253.'),
301	('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'Microbes always produce methane.
302	(if there is no methane, no microbes)'),
303	('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'It''s very annoying to find the Conclusion here because none of the claims sound obviously like an opinion, and there aren''t argument indicator words otherwise helping to guide us.
304	The one indicator word we get is the Since. Any time we see,
305	Since _____ , _____ .
306	it means that the Since-claim is support for the second claim, which is a conclusion.
307	But there''s no way the conclusion we''re supposed to be proving with the correct answer is the second half of that last sentence.
308	Why? Because that''s already a logically guaranteed conclusion. All they''re doing in that last sentence is saying, "Since X → Y, if there''s not Y then there''s not X."
309	Since microbes always produce methane
310	Micr → Meth
311	We know if there''s no methane, no microbes
312	~Meth → ~Micr
313	Okay, so the final sentence is just a statement and its contrapositive. That can''t be the conclusion we need to prove.
314	The first sentence is just a factual claim about current evidence. So by process of elimination, we need to be considering the 2nd sentence the main conclusion.
315	The author is trying to prove that "if there''s no methane, there''s no life", which would look like this, if we elongated the arrow to show the intended logic path:
316	```
317	CONCLUSION
318	~methane ------------------------> ~life
319	EVIDENCE
320	~methane ----> ~microbes
321	MISSING PIECE OF THE PATH
322	~microbes ----> ~life
323	```'),
324	('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Let''s find an answer saying, "If there are no microbes, then there''s no life", or its contrapositive, "Wherever there is life, there are microbes".
325	Since we need to prove a claim about "no life" but the evidence never spoke about that, the correct answer absolute must be a claim that allows us to prove "no life". We can summarily reject any answer that doesn''t mention Life or any answer that would only let you prove "there is life".')
326	) AS s(qid, style, position, label, body)
327	JOIN public.lr_questions lq ON lq.qid = s.qid AND lq.preptest = 154
328	JOIN public.lr_explanations ex ON ex.question_id = lq.id AND ex.style = s.style
329	ON CONFLICT (explanation_id, position) DO NOTHING
330	RETURNING explanation_id;

## assistant

Tool call: mcp__d2692868-3949-490b-864d-de2c41e9f9b7__query_database
{"project_id": "53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76", "sql": "WITH ins AS (\nINSERT INTO public.lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id, position, label, body)\nSELECT ex.id, s.position, s.label, s.body\nFROM (VALUES\n('PT154-S4-Q1', 'single', 0, 'Opposing Idea', 'One reason swimming immediately after eating is thought to be dangerous is that it could cause muscle cramps.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q1', 'single', 1, 'Conclusion', 'There is no reason to believe this [swimming immediately after eating could cause muscle cramps].'),\n('PT154-S4-Q1', 'single', 2, 'Evidence', 'Muscle cramps are usually caused by muscle fatigue and dehydration, which are unrelated to eating.\nReduced blood flow to muscles during digestion might also be a cause [of muscle cramps], though this is disputed.\nIn any case, not enough blood goes to the stomach to aid in digestion after a meal to reduce blood flow to muscles.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q1', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate / Goal', 'This argument follows a common pattern. A claim or idea is stated in the first sentence. The next sentence pivots to a contrasting idea. That contrasting idea is the main point of the argument—the conclusion. The rest of the argument contains evidence supporting that conclusion.\nThe conclusion is in the second sentence of this argument. It claims that there is no reason to believe that swimming immediately after eating causes muscle cramps. We''ll look for a correct answer that makes this same claim.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q2', 'single', 0, 'Flores'' Claims', '1. The behavior of the character Gawain in Malory''s Le Morte D''Arthur fluctuates radically and without apparent reason.\n2. Gawain fluctuates between heroic and criminal.\n3. Malory''s portrayal of Gawain is incoherent.\n4. Malory portrays Gawain this way because Malory based his book on earlier sources from different eras.\n5. Gawain''s role changed over time.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q2', 'single', 1, 'Piro''s Responses', '1. Disagrees\n2. Agrees\n3. Disagrees\n4. Agrees\n5. Agrees'),\n('PT154-S4-Q2', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Piro agrees with most of the points that Flores makes, but does not agree that Gawain''s behavior is \"without apparent reason\" or \"incoherent.\" According to Piro, Gawain''s behavior was influenced by the type of situation he was in. Gawain behaved one way during crises, and a different way during stable periods.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q2', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We''ll look for an answer that addresses claim #1 or #3.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q3', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'The hearing-loss people think is from old age is really from accumulated damage of long-term exposure to loud noise.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q3', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Remote populations who have had little exposure to loud noise have almost no \"age-related\" hearing loss.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q3', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author is considering the Curious Question, \"what causes the hearing loss we observe in old people\" and eschewing the common Explanation that \"old age causes the hearing loss\".\nThe author offers an Alternate Explanation: \"accumulation of long-term exposure to loud noise is what causes the hearing loss\".\nShe strengthens her Hypothesis with a classic \"No Cause, No Effect\" piece of evidence:\npopulations not exposed to long-term noise do not show hearing loss in old people.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q3', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since this is Strengthen, we will probably add some more credence to the Author''s Explanation that long-term exposure to noise is causing hearing loss at an old age:\n- maybe an example where \"people with LOTS of exposure were even MORE likely to have hearing damage when old\"\n- maybe an explanation of the causal mechanism that connects exposure to noise to hearing loss\nIt could also rule-out an Alternate Explanation for why the remote populations have little hearing loss (\"they do not die so early that they never get to be as old as the ''age-related'' hearing loss in other cultures\")'),\n('PT154-S4-Q4', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (therefore)', 'The law is inadequate as a means of giving the English people access to their archaeological heritage.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q4', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The law requires finders of valuable antiquities to offer them to English museums at reasonable prices.\nIt failed to apply to the Roman helmet that was recently discovered in England (and sold to a private collector), because the helmet is neither prehistoric nor made of precious metal.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q4', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'There is a New Concept in the Conclusion, because it''s talking about whether a law is / isn''t an adequate means of giving the English people access to their archaeological heritage.\nWhat do we mean by \"archaeological heritage\"? What is the author assuming would be included there?\nWhat do we mean by \"an adequate means of giving the English people access\"? How much access are the English people entitled to? What makes a means of access adequate or inadequate?\nWe know that the author is peeved at this law. Why? Had the law applied to the bronze helmet, then the helmet would have to be offered to an English museum. Since the law didn''t apply, the helmet was sold to a private collector.\nThe author is thinking that, \"Since this helmet ended up in the hands of a private collector, not a museum, this law failed to be an adequate way of giving the English people access to their archaeological heritage\".'),\n('PT154-S4-Q4', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'There are a couple Missing Links / Ideas here.\n1. The author is assuming that this bronze helmet is part of the English people''s archaeological heritage.\n2. The author is assuming that if such an item from their heritage ends up with a private collector rather than a museum, then the English people don''t have adequate access to it.\nWe might otherwise see answers Defending against an objection, such as:\n- the Roman bronze helmet is not more properly considered to be part of the archaeological heritage of the Italian people\n- private collectors do not give the English more access to any antiquities they own than do English museums.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q5', 'single', 0, 'Given that...', 'Some people subscribe to newspapers that provide extensive coverage of election issues.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q5', 'single', 1, 'How can it be that...', 'Studies show that they are no better informed about election issues than subscribers to newspapers that have very little coverage of these issues.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q5', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'This question is comparing two groups of newspaper subscribers. One group subscribes to newspapers that provide extensive coverage of election issues. The other group subscribes to newspapers that have very little coverage of election issues. We would expect the first group to be better informed about these issues. But surprisingly, studies show that they aren''t better informed.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q5', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Expect a correct answer to provide an additional fact about the first group which would get in the way of them being better informed, even if they subscribe to papers offering more extensive coverage of the issues.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (So / probably)', 'If people in industrialized societies undertook corresponding group tasks, their communities would probably become more close-knit.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 1, 'Intermediate Conclusion', 'Much of what made medieval European communities as close-knit as they usually were was the way they went about meeting basic needs.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 2, 'Evidence', 'In medieval communities, to get crops harvested or to dig a well, people had to come together in respectful cooperation, despite any private grievances they might have had.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'On Main Conclusion and Role, the main conclusion is earlier than the evidence about 85-90% of the time (it''s usually the very first sentence or attached to some pivot/rebuttal).\nBut here we looking at the 10% of the time where the Conclusion is actually the last claim in the paragraph, on a Main Conclusion question. (This tends to only happen on easier Main Conclusion questions early in the LR section, such as Q6 like this one)\nWe know the word \"So\" indicates conclusions. The word \"probably\" often indicates an Opinion. Both of those words show up in the final sentence.\nWhen we think we''ve found the Conclusion, we should test whether it is actually supported, by asking why should we believe that?\nWhy should we believe that we modern industrialized societies would probably become more close-knit if we undertook group tasks with the community?\nBecause ... much of what made Medieval societies close-knit was the group tasks they undertook together (such as harvesting crops or digging a well). It forced them to come together in respectful cooperation, suspending any private grievances.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q6', 'single', 4, 'Goal', 'Okay, we confirmed that the last sentence is the conclusion. It is wearing a conclusion indicator (So); it is expressing an opinion the author has (probably); and it is supported by the other two claims in the paragraph.\nSo let''s go find an answer that matches the meaning of the final sentence.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 0, 'Evidence', 'Applying a copper solution to blighted plants is an effective treatment for the disease [late blight].'),\n('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 1, 'Conclusion (Therefore)', 'Late blight must be caused by a deficiency of copper in the plant''s mineral intake.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'LR questions often contain arguments that contain evidence about a correlation—two things that tend to happen together. Based on just that correlation, the argument claims that one of the things causes another. This is called a Causal Flaw, which is often explained by saying \"a correlation does not prove causation.\"\nThis argument''s conclusion claims that late blight is caused by a copper deficiency. But the evidence is a bit stronger than a mere correlation. The copper solution is described as an \"effective treatment.\" This indicates that the copper solution causes the plants to be cured of the disease.\nSo, what''s the exact flaw here? The conclusion implies that tomato plants need copper. The assumption is that the copper solution provides the plants with this copper that they need. But none of that is stated in the evidence. We''re never told what the copper solution actually does, only that it works. It certainly might cure a copper deficiency, but there are other, equally plausible alternative explanations.\nFor example, late blight could be caused by some type of organism that is killed by the copper solution. If this is true, the conclusion doesn''t really make sense. It would be the same as saying, \"Antibiotic cream is an effective treatment for an infected wound. Therefore, infected wounds are caused by a deficiency of antibiotic cream in a person''s diet.\"'),\n('PT154-S4-Q7', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer with evidence that describes some type of causal connection without explaining exactly how that connection works. The conclusion will be based on an assumption that there is only one explanation, when other reasonable possibilities exist. Watch out for answers containing arguments that aren''t flawed, or ones that contain a completely different flaw.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q8', 'single', 0, 'Given', 'At BigFoods, we compare prices for you.\nWe recently determined which items our shoppers buy most often.\nA varied sample of these items cost 10 percent more at Grocerytown than at BigFoods!\nNone of these regularly purchased grocery items were on sale at BigFoods.\nThese are our everyday prices!'),\n('PT154-S4-Q8', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'Are you hearing these statements in a typical television commercial announcer''s voice? You don''t get any extra points on the LSAT for that, but it''s entertaining to read it that way.\nWhat stands out most in this information is the comparison between BigFoods and Grocerytown. We know that some items normally cost 10 percent more at Grocerytown. We know that these items are some of the ones that shoppers at BigFoods buy most often.\nThe type of answer that can be strongly supported by these statements is likely to be something relatively unexciting and closely related to what we already know. It might be something like, \"people who shop at BigFoods will spend more money on items that they regularly purchase if they shop at Grocerytown instead.\" Even though this sounds like it''s just repeating the information we were given, the stimulus never explicitly made this exact statement.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q8', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'We''ll expect a correct answer to be very similar to the information that we were given, perhaps summarizing or explicitly connecting two or more of the given statements. Beware of answers that are too strong or ones that describe related topics that were never actually mentioned in the given statements.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q9', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Even if doctors'' advising their patients to change diet/exercise rarely results in weight loss, that''s no reason for doctors to abandon giving that advice.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q9', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'Changes in diet/exercise can have other beneficial effects.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q9', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'It''s not really clear from the text whether people went to their doctors and said, \"What do you suggest for weight loss?\" and then the doctors suggested diet/exercise changes, even though they rarely work as a solution.\nIt''s presented more as though doctors just advocate changes in diet and exercise, and we aren''t sure what their motivation for doing so was.\nHad it been specifically advice for weight loss, then I''d probably anticipate a principle like, \"It''s okay to suggest an activity as a solution for someone''s problem, even if the activity rarely works as a solution to that problem, as long as the activity would provide some other benefit.\"'),\n('PT154-S4-Q9', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since we don''t know whether the doctors were giving bad weight loss advice, we should probably just keep our principle free of that language and just describe what we were told:\nJust because Action Y usually fails to do Thing A doesn''t mean we should stop suggesting it. After all, it does good things other than Thing A.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Mountain climbing isn''t as dangerous as the popular media likes to claim.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'From 1922 - 2002, there were less than 200 climbing fatalities on Mount Everest, which is one of the most dangerous mountains in the world. In 2002, alone, there were 7,000 traffic fatalities in France.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Given this crazy asymmetry between deaths on Everest vs. those on the roadways of France, how could we still argue that mountain climbing is as dangerous as the popular media has said (which we''ll assume is \"quite dangerous\"?)\nOne move we have is saying, \"Cool, well the roadways in France are crazy dangerous, so no wonder there are so many deaths. Just because mountain climbing isn''t as dangerous, that doesn''t mean it''s not dangerous!\"\nIf you claimed that the Empire State Building in NYC was a really tall building, and someone said \"No, it''s not. There''s a couple buildings in Dubai that are taller\", that''s not a good objection.\nThe popular media didn''t say mountain climbing is the most dangerous thing ever, so pointing out that something else is potentially more dangerous is a Relative vs. Absolute type flaw.\nBut we should also think about how unfair the comparison is. If I tried to argue that \"skydiving is more dangerous than flying in an airplane\", and you said, \"Nuh-uh — more people die in airplanes each year\", it''s not a fair comparison.\nWe don''t measure danger in terms of the raw number of injuries / fatalities. We judge danger in terms of the likelihood of getting an injury / fatality.\nIf 13% of skydivers die each year, but only 1% of airplane riders die, then it seems like skydiving is more dangerous, regardless of the absolute number of deaths. There will be more deaths from airplane riders, since there are hundreds of millions of people who fly each year, whereas there might only be 1 million people who skydive each year.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q10', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We have two possible objections:\n- \"Just because one thing is even more dangerous doesn''t prove the first thing isn''t plenty dangerous itself\"\n- \"What if the relative rate of deaths is much higher for Everest, and the absolute number is lower just because there are only a few hundred people who try to climb Everest each year?\"'),\n('PT154-S4-Q11', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Gyms and fitness centers are sometimes good places to buy used exercise machines.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q11', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'When they upgrade their machines, they sell their old ones used at reasonable prices. They''ve been used a bunch, but they are sturdier than normal home machines and they are usually well-maintained.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q11', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The language we need our principle to strengthen is that G''s and FC''s are sometimes good places to buy X.\nWhy? Because the prices are reasonable and the machines are sturdy and well-maintained.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q11', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'If we''re pre-phrasing a typical conditional answer, it would sound something like\n```\nplace gives reasonable price on X\nor can be\nplace sells sturdier versions of X → good place\nor       to buy X\nplace sells well-maintained X''s\n```\nIf we''re pre-phrasing a should-strengthener, then we want an answer saying that in buying used exercise machines, you should focus on reasonable price, well-made, or well-maintained.\nIf we''re pre-phrasing a \"weight the competing concerns\" answer (since this argument did have competing interests), we''d want something like it''s more important that it''s reasonably priced / better built / well-maintained than that it''s been used very little.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q12', 'single', 0, 'Given', 'A lichen is made up of a photosynthetic organism and a fungus growing in symbiosis on a solid surface.\nLichens absorb minerals from air and rainwater but also from the surfaces on which they grow.\nThey cannot excrete the elements they absorb.\nSome varieties are very vulnerable to toxic compounds, including compounds found in polluted air.\nSuch compounds can damage both of the symbiotic partners.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q12', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate / Goal', 'We''re given a bunch of information about lichens, but there aren''t conditional or quantified statements that can clearly be linked together. The best approach here is to dive into the answers and compare them to the given statements. We should keep an eye open for answers that seem related to these statements but are actually unsupported or out of scope.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q13', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion (Thus / conclude)', 'If the poets I''ve met are representative of poets generally, one can reasonably conclude that many poets are made melancholy by writing poetry.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q13', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'The poets I have met are more often disposed to melancholy, and an activity as profound and engrossing as writing poetry can be depressing.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q13', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author supports his conclusion that \"writing poetry makes many poets melancholy\" with two pieces of info:\n1. the sample of poets he knows mostly include melancholy poets\n2. there''s a reason why there would be a plausible causal path from writing poetry to being melancholy (poetry --> profound and engrossing activity --> can be depressing --> melancholy).'),\n('PT154-S4-Q13', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'The question stem is asking us about the final claim, with is a Supporting Idea. The people who are scared of COVID aren''t leaving their house, so you''re not getting a chance to interview them.\"\nOne of the ten famous flaws is Sampling, when an author relies on a sample that may be too small, self-selecting, or unrepresentative.\nThe store owner here seems to be relying on a self-selecting sample.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q19', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer pointing out the sampling bias of asking people who came to the store \"are you too scared to come to the store\", or for an answer pointing out the objection that \"customers who are afraid of crime in the neighborhood will have stopped coming to your store\".'),\n('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'No serious novelist is motivated primarily by the desire to make money.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'All serious novelists care about literary style.\nThe more a novel appeals to the general public, the more money its author will make.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'CONVERSATIONAL TAKE\nThe conclusion is making a connection between the concepts of \"serious novelist\" and \"desire to make money\".\nSerious novelist is a concept the evidence defines / discusses. We know that more appealing novels make more money. So if you desired money, we know you''d try to make more appealing novels.\nIf you didn''t desire money, you wouldn''t care if you novels were appealing.\n\"Someone who cares about literary style is not motivated by the desire to (does not care if they don''t) make money.\"\nSo, someone who cares about literary style doesn''t care if their novels are appealing? Yes, the author must be thinking that serious novelists care about literary style, which they know the general public doesn''t find appealing, so their books aren''t as likely to make money. So the author infers that serious novelists must not be motivated by a desire to make money.\nFORMAL TAKE\nThe conclusion defines this logic path as our objective:\n```\nSerious --------------------------> not primarily\nNovelist       motiv by $\n```\nThe premise gives us this part of the path:\n```\nSerious -----> cares about\nNovelist       literary style\n```\nSo the missing ingredient the answer needs to provide to complete our logic path:\n```\ncares about ----> not primarily\nliterary style       motiv by $\n```'),\n('PT154-S4-Q20', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'We need to prove \"primary motivation is not money\" about serious novelists, and the only we know for sure about serious novelists is that they care about literary style. more ways there are in which it can fail.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q21', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'The author is essentially making a move from \"there are more ways the system can fail\" to \"it is more likely that the system will fail\".\nMathematically, that doesn''t have to be true.\nSay there are only two ways to fail, and each way of failing has about a 30% chance of occurring.\nAvoiding failure would mean that both things go right, which is 70% probability times 70% probability\n7/10 * 7/10 = 49/100 = 49% chance it doesn''t fail.\nNo say there are four ways to fail, but each of them only has about ineffective.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q22', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'We have an Anti-Causal conclusion, so we''ll be trying to argue that \"environmental factors DO have much effect on whether teens participate in sports\".\nWe can weaken the argument by \"making excuses\" for the author''s premises or by introducing new evidence that helps us say \"environmental factors do have a lot of effect\".\nGiven That\nWithin a family, some teens play sports and others don''t\nHow Could We Argue That\nfamily life is operating as an environmental factor?\nWe would say a number of things:\n- who says that the family is trying to influence all its kids in the same direction? (maybe you have an old-school Dad who tries to move his son towards sports but moves his daughters away from sports).\n- what if the younger siblings want to differentiate themselves from the older sibling that got into sports?\nGiven That\nSchool programs that try to get inactive teens into playing sports usually don''t work,\nHow Could We Argue That\nenvironmental factors do influence whether teens play sports?\nMaybe we could say that these cheesy assemblies exert influence, just in an opposite, backfiring direction. Maybe teens are being convinced out of getting into high school sports based on who unappealing these school programs are.\nOr we could just say that these programs are poorly designed and that if properly designed they would be able to exert some environmental influence.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q22', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Look for an answer \"explaining away\" either of the two premises in a way that protects the idea that environmental factors are playing a role, or for any answer that helps us argue that environmental factors are playing a role.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Talking on a cell phone while driving is more dangerous than talking with someone in the vehicle while driving.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 1, 'Intermediate Conclusion', 'The person on the phone can''t immediately recognize when driving is becoming difficult.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 2, 'Evidence', 'The person on the phone can''t see the driving situation. Meanwhile, a conversation partner inside the vehicle will usually shut up or say something helpful if it''s a difficult driving situation.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 3, 'Evaluate', 'There is a missing link in going from the first half of the second sentence to the second half of the second sentence:\nif you can''t see a driving situation, then you can''t immediately recognize when driving becomes difficult.\nThis is pretty reasonable, but one could attack its veracity — maybe you can hear a difference in the person you''re talking to and that tells you that they are confronting a difficult driving situation.\nNevertheless, it''s probably not that likely that LSAC is testing that assumption.\nThe bigger gap would be getting from \"what''s different\" between cell phone conversation and in-car conversation to \"more / less dangerous\".'),\n('PT154-S4-Q23', 'single', 4, 'Goal', 'The author is assuming that \"if conversation partner keeps talking during difficult driving situation, then it''s more dangerous\", or, equivalently, \"if someone shuts up or offers helpful warnings during a difficult driving situation, then it''s less dangerous.\"'),\n('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'Experiencing a traumatic event can affect how much cortisol one produces in response to stress.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 1, 'Evidence', 'People who have experienced trauma (w/o PTSD) tend to produce more cortisol when exposed to stress than do people who haven''t experienced trauma.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'Since the evidence is a Curious Comparison and the conclusion is inferring some sort of Causal claim, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset.\nCurious Fact ... Why is it that people who have had a traumatic event produce higher levels of cortisol when exposed to stress?\nAuthor''s Explanation ... Experiencing a traumatic event apparently changed how much cortisol their bodies produce in response to stress.\nThe two pressure points are always\n- Alternate Explanations for the curious fact\n- the plausibility of the Author''s Explanation\nHow about that whole PTSD thing. Did that matter?\nHard to say. It certainly feels like a detail-clue they wouldn''t accidentally put in there if they weren''t planning to use it.\nPeople who experienced a traumatic event but do subsequently develop PTSD are not involved in the curious comparison.\nThere are three groups, but we''re only comparing the first two:\nno trauma | trauma, no PTSD | trauma, yes PTSD'),\n('PT154-S4-Q24', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Since this is Weaken, the correct answer is more likely to be an Alternate Explanation for why people who have experienced trauma tend to produce more cortisol in response to stress.\nBut it might undermine the plausibility that experiencing the trauma changed their cortisol response.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q25', 'single', 0, 'Statements', 'From 1960 to 2010, the fishing industry was able to harvest a greater percentage of the total amount of fish, by weight, in the world''s oceans, in each succeeding year.\nThe harvest, by weight, increased steadily from 1960 to 1995, but didn''t increase after 1995.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q25', 'single', 1, 'Evaluate', 'We usually read Must Be True looking for Conditional, Causal, or Math-y type ideas. This one seems to giving us Math-y ideas, so reconciling the first and second sentences will give us some mathematical inference.\nWe know that the fishing industry caught a higher percentage of the weight of the fish in the ocean each year.\nLet''s pretend there are 1,000 tons of fish in the ocean each year.\n```\n       % caught   tons caught\n1960   10%        100\n1965   11%        110\n1970   12%        120\n....\n1995   20%        200\n2000   25%        200\n2005   50%        200\n```\nWe complied with two different constraints there:\n1. percentage keeps increasing from 1960 to 2010\n2. overall weight of fish caught increases from 1960 to 1995\n3. overall weight of fish caught stops increasing from 1995 to 2010\nBut notice something funky with our numbers. For a while they kept equaling 1000 tons of fish in the sea, but once the percentage goes up and the tonnage caught stays the same, it means that the total weight of fish in the sea has to start decreasing.\n```\n       % caught   tons caught   total in sea\n1960   10%        100           1000\n1965   11%        110           1000\n1970   12%        120           1000\n....\n1995   20%        200           1000\n2000   25%        200           800\n2005   50%        200           400\n```\nThe inference they''re going for here is the same as this:\nmy rent has stayed the same for the past 10 years, but nowadays my rent is a bigger percentage of my income.\nWhat can we infer?\nMy income is lower now than it used to be.\nIf I''m paying a higher tax rate this year than last year, but the government is still getting the same money from me, then that means my taxable income has gone down.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q25', 'single', 2, 'Goal', 'We don''t know for sure what''s going on from 1960 through 1995. It''s possible that the overall weight of fish in the ocean goes down, stays the same, or goes up.\nBut once we get to 1995, we know for sure that the total weight of fish in the ocean starts going down, because the % of available fish weight being caught keeps rising but the actual weight of fish being caught does not increase.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 0, 'Conclusion', 'If there''s no methane on Planet 253, then there''s no life on Planet 253.'),\n('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 1, 'Evidence (since)', 'Microbes always produce methane.\n(if there is no methane, no microbes)'),\n('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 2, 'Evaluate', 'It''s very annoying to find the Conclusion here because none of the claims sound obviously like an opinion, and there aren''t argument indicator words otherwise helping to guide us.\nThe one indicator word we get is the Since. Any time we see,\nSince _____ , _____ .\nit means that the Since-claim is support for the second claim, which is a conclusion.\nBut there''s no way the conclusion we''re supposed to be proving with the correct answer is the second half of that last sentence.\nWhy? Because that''s already a logically guaranteed conclusion. All they''re doing in that last sentence is saying, \"Since X → Y, if there''s not Y then there''s not X.\"\nSince microbes always produce methane\nMicr → Meth\nWe know if there''s no methane, no microbes\n~Meth → ~Micr\nOkay, so the final sentence is just a statement and its contrapositive. That can''t be the conclusion we need to prove.\nThe first sentence is just a factual claim about current evidence. So by process of elimination, we need to be considering the 2nd sentence the main conclusion.\nThe author is trying to prove that \"if there''s no methane, there''s no life\", which would look like this, if we elongated the arrow to show the intended logic path:\n```\nCONCLUSION\n~methane ------------------------> ~life\nEVIDENCE\n~methane ----> ~microbes\nMISSING PIECE OF THE PATH\n~microbes ----> ~life\n```'),\n('PT154-S4-Q26', 'single', 3, 'Goal', 'Let''s find an answer saying, \"If there are no microbes, then there''s no life\", or its contrapositive, \"Wherever there is life, there are microbes\".\nSince we need to prove a claim about \"no life\" but the evidence never spoke about that, the correct answer absolute must be a claim that allows us to prove \"no life\". We can summarily reject any answer that doesn''t mention Life or any answer that would only let you prove \"there is life\".')\n) AS s(qid, style, position, label, body)\nJOIN public.lr_questions lq ON lq.qid = s.qid AND lq.preptest = 154\nJOIN public.lr_explanations ex ON ex.question_id = lq.id AND ex.style = s.style\nON CONFLICT (explanation_id, position) DO NOTHING\nRETURNING 1)\nSELECT count(*) AS inserted FROM ins;"}

**Why the plan was wrong:** I'd said PT156 and PT159 had transcribed files that were never loaded. In fact, those files hold only the 15 and 14 questions LSAT Lab wrote explanations for. The rest were video-only, and LSAT Lab's own progress notes confirm it. Everything in those files was already in the database. Their 73 gaps have no text anywhere, so they join the list Claude writes.

**PT154 is fixed and live.**
- The explanations were in the file, but written in a slightly different layout. The script that reads these files dropped the walkthroughs and loaded only the answer-choice notes.
- I fixed the script and added a test. I ran it on every explanation file: the only output that changes is PT154's. That's in [PR #138](https://github.com/vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db/pull/138), which isn't merged yet.
- I loaded the missing walkthroughs straight into the live database: 74 questions. I checked every row against the source file, and all of them match exactly.
- Nothing already in the database was changed or overwritten. I saved a copy of PT154's rows from before the load in `raw/sources/lsatu-lr-snapshots/`.
- The other 3 PT154 questions (S2 Q3–Q5) never had a written walkthrough, so they stay on Claude's list.

**Where coverage stands**

| State | Questions |
|---|---|
| Unexplained (no explanation at all) | 354 |
| Notes-only (answer-choice notes, no walkthrough) | 224 (was 298) |
| Unloaded (not in the explanation tables) | 194 |
| **Missing in total** | **772 of 3,965** (was 846) |

Both coverage pages are regenerated. They now come from a script, [system/scripts/lr-explanation-coverage.py](system/scripts/lr-explanation-coverage.py), so you can rerun it after each batch.

**Next is step 3: proposing the golden set of about 25 explanations for you to approve.** PT154 turns out to be a strong candidate source. Its walkthroughs use concrete examples, work through the conditional logic, and end each explanation with a clear prediction of the answer.

The brainstorm capture, [brainstorms/2026-09-26-lr-explanation-coverage.md](brainstorms/2026-09-26-lr-explanation-coverage.md), now has a record of what was done, including this correction.

## user

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\Joshu\.claude\skills\session-handoff

# Session Handoff

Produce a repeatable end-of-session summary so Joshua can `/clear` and start a fresh agent without losing continuity. The next agent should pick up by reading this summary alone.

This is a **context-handoff artifact**, not a status report. The audience is a future instance of you, not a stakeholder.

**Handoff vs. wrapup:** run `session-handoff` when the *context window* is the problem — nearing ~250k tokens (the dumb zone), or passing work between Claude and Codex. It is chat-only and writes nothing. Run `wrapup` when the *work day* is done — it strikes tasks, refreshes `wiki/hot.md`, and appends `wiki/log.md`. They can be run back to back (wrapup to persist, then handoff to reset).

## When to invoke

Joshua says: "session handoff", "hand off", "handoff summary", "summarize before I clear", or any near-equivalent. Note "let's wrap up" belongs to `wrapup`, not here — if he says that and the context window is also deep, run `wrapup` first, then offer this. Also invoke proactively if he says he's about to `/clear` without having run it, or if the session is clearly deep into the dumb zone.

## How to produce the summary

1. **Review the full conversation**, not just the last few turns. Handoffs miss things when they only summarize recent context.
2. **Pull state from these sources (in order):**
   - Plan files referenced this session (check `~/.claude/plans/` if a plan was mentioned).
   - TodoWrite state — any in-progress or pending tasks.
   - Background processes started with `run_in_background` — shell IDs are load-bearing for the next agent.
   - Files created or modified this session — you know what you touched; don't grep to re-discover.
   - Memory files written or updated (`~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/`).
   - Unresolved questions — things you asked Joshua that never got a clear answer, or things he asked that got deflected.
3. **Do NOT audit the filesystem.** This is synthesis of what happened in THIS session. No `git log`, no broad `Glob` sweeps. If you didn't touch it this session, it doesn't belong here.
4. **Produce the output in chat.** Do not write a file. Do not update memory. Chat-only.

## Output template — use exactly this structure, every time

```
# Session Handoff — <one-line title of what this session was about>

## Where it started
<2-3 sentences: what Joshua asked for, key framing or constraints that emerged>

## Decisions locked + what shipped
- <decision or change> — <why, and where it lives (absolute path if a file)>
- ...

## Key files for next session
- `<absolute path>` — <why the next agent should read this first>
- Plan file: `<path>` (if a plan drove the session)
- Memory files touched: `<paths>` (if any)

## Running state
- Background processes: <shell IDs + what they are + how to kill> — or "none"
- Dev servers / ports: <url + port> — or "none"
- Open worktrees / branches: <paths> — or "none"

## Verified state as of <HH:MM>
- <claim about live/system state> — <how it was checked, and when>
- <claim> — NOT re-verified this session; last confirmed <when>
- ...

## Verification — how to confirm things still work
- `<command>` — <expected outcome>
- ...

## Deferred + open questions
- Deferred: <item> — <why pushed to later>
- OPEN (must be resolved or re-asked before new work): <question needing Joshua's input> — <context>

## Pick up here
<1-2 sentences: the single most likely next action for a fresh agent>
```

## Hard rules

1. **Chat output only.** Never write the handoff to a file. Never update memory from this skill. (That is `wrapup`'s job, not this one.)
2. **Never invent state.** If a section has nothing to report, write "none" — do not omit the section. Structure stability is the whole point.
3. **Absolute paths always.** The next agent may have a different working directory, or be Codex.
4. **If a plan file drove the session, name it first** in "Key files" so the next agent reads it before anything else.
5. **No emojis, no hype, no "great job" summaries.** Terse and concrete — paths, commands, shell IDs, decisions. Tone of a seasoned engineer handing off at end-of-shift.
6. **Background process IDs are critical.** If you started any `run_in_background` shells, their IDs must appear in "Running state" with the kill command — the next agent cannot find them otherwise.
7. **Stamp verified state, and separate it from assumed state.** Run `date "+%H:%M"` and use the real time. Every claim about live systems (deploy status, DB contents, what was sent, what is published) is either something you actually checked this session — say how — or explicitly marked NOT re-verified. A handoff that presents stale belief as current state makes the next session re-derive everything, which is the exact cost this skill exists to remove.
8. **Open questions are obligations, not notes.** Anything under OPEN must be resolved by the next session or re-asked to Joshua before it starts new work. Requirements have been silently re-typed across sessions, and a promised artifact once never arrived because nobody re-asked. If a question was open in the handoff you are picking up from, it is the first thing you raise.

## Anti-patterns — do not do these

- Summarizing the last 3 turns and calling it a handoff.
- Listing files by relative path.
- Skipping "Running state" because "nothing is running" — write "none" instead.
- Writing the summary to any file. This is chat-only by design.
- Adding a "what went well / what went poorly" retrospective. This isn't a retro.
- Recommending next steps beyond the single "Pick up here" line. The next agent decides; you just hand off.
